4 Data Engineering

Lecture slides for this chapter: Part 1 · Part 2

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

Why does training data function like source code in a machine learning system?

In conventional software, programmers write logic that computers execute. In machine learning, programmers write optimization procedures that extract operational logic from data. This inversion makes training data resemble source code: changing it can change the learned system when the model is retrained, even if no traditional code changes. Yet data rarely receives the review, testing, and version control that even a one-line code change routinely receives. Subtle labeling inconsistencies can induce behavioral inconsistencies; missing edge cases can leave corresponding failures unmeasured; historical biases can propagate into learned behavior. A model trained only on that dataset cannot infer task-relevant evidence absent from it, and systematic label errors can shape what it learns. Unlike traditional source code, which sits inert until a programmer modifies it, the operating distribution can shift as the world changes, potentially changing production performance even when nothing in the codebase has been touched. Data engineering can consume substantial project effort, often more than modeling itself, because the work is consequential. A labeling error caught at collection costs a relabel; the same error found after deployment can cost a retraining run, an incident investigation, and the trust of the people the system failed. Every decision in the data pipeline (what to collect, how to label, when to filter, how to split) propagates forward to constrain model architecture, training dynamics, and deployment viability. Data engineering is therefore not preprocessing but programming in a different language, one where quality control, versioning, and monitoring determine whether the compiled system works today and continues working tomorrow. In D·A·M terms, data overlaps both the algorithm and the machine; it bounds what the model can learn, and the pipeline that delivers it bounds how fast the machine can learn it.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain data as source code and trace how data cascades propagate through ML systems
  • Evaluate acquisition strategies against coverage, quality, labeling cost, governance, and deployment constraints
  • Design ingestion and validation pipelines for batch, streaming, ETL, and ELT workloads
  • Specify labeling, transformation, and release practices that prevent data leakage and preserve label quality, training-serving consistency, and reproducibility
  • Calculate data gravity, data-stall, and storage-bandwidth costs for moving or serving datasets
  • Select storage tiers, file formats, and data loader designs that keep the accelerator fed
  • Diagnose drift, data debt, and production pipeline failures using drift metrics, debt indicators, and controlled comparisons of pipeline versions
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

4.1 Data as Code

A missing accent in a speech corpus, a mislabeled class, or a feature computed one way in training and another in serving becomes a defect in system behavior with no line of code to point to. Because a machine learning system learns its behavior from data, the trained model is a compressed summary of its training set. It can represent only the evidence that data contains, and it treats the data’s labels as the definition of a correct answer. The bitter lesson (The Bitter Lesson) explains why this dependence has grown rather than shrunk. Methods that learn from data at scale have repeatedly overtaken methods built on hand-encoded knowledge, so the knowledge that engineers once wrote into programs must now arrive through data. In the lifecycle that ML Lifecycle lays out, this work is Stage 2, which ends by handing a versioned dataset to model development; every later stage inherits what that dataset contains and what it lacks. Across 668 notable models published since 1950 (figure 1), training dataset sizes show how far that shift has gone (Epoch AI 2026). Until 2010, dataset size doubled roughly every 34 months; after 2010, as deep learning and web-scale collection took hold, it doubled about every 8 months, climbing from tens of thousands of examples in early vision benchmarks past the million-image scale of ImageNet to trillions of tokens in modern language models.

At that scale, data constrains the machine as well as the model. Every training step reads its examples from storage, decodes them, and moves them to the accelerator, and an accelerator waiting on input computes nothing however fast it is. Datasets measured in terabytes also have physical mass. They are slow and expensive to move, and they spend energy at every level of the memory hierarchy they cross. Data therefore occupies two of the pairwise overlaps of the D·A·M taxonomy at once, the ones labeled what to learn from and how to move information in The D·A·M Intersection Landscape. Toward the algorithm, it bounds what the model can learn; toward the machine, it bounds how fast learning can proceed.

Both bounds are expensive to meet. Cleaning and organizing data was the most common answer to where data scientists spend their time in the practitioner survey that ML Lifecycle presents (CrowdFlower 2016). In the image-classification cost comparison later in this chapter, labeling a dataset costs roughly 2,500× to 5,000× as much as one training run on it. Each stage between raw input and an ML-ready dataset (acquisition, ingestion and validation, labeling, and processing) can also introduce a defect that no later stage detects. Data bounds both capability and throughput and often dominates cost, so it requires the same engineering discipline as the model and the machine: acquisition systems, validation checks, labeling workflows, storage layouts, and governance.

CrowdFlower. 2016. Data Science Report 2016.
Definition 4.1: Data engineering

Data engineering is the infrastructure layer that manages the lifecycle of data from source to model, encompassing acquisition, transformation, storage, and governance.

  1. Significance: It sets both bounds that data places on an ML system. Dataset composition, coverage, and labels determine whether the logical dataset (\(\DatasetSize\), the \(\text{Samples/Task}\) factor of the iron law) represents the target distribution, and therefore what the model can learn. In the unpacked iron law, the pipeline governs the physical bytes moved \((\DataVolume/\Bandwidth)\), and therefore how fast learning can proceed.
  2. Distinction: Unlike data science, which focuses on inference and insight, data engineering addresses the scalability and reliability of the data pipeline.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception reduces data engineering to mere “data cleaning.” In systems terms, it is a build pipeline that turns raw, noisy observations into the validated, versioned tensors that accelerator runtimes consume.

↩︎ The iron law of ML systems, whose data-movement term this chapter governs, is established in Iron Law of ML Systems.

The data-as-code principle (1) turns the model’s dependence on its data into an engineering rule. Changing a dataset changes the trained model just as changing source code changes a program, even when no line of algorithmic code is touched. The pipeline that produces a dataset is therefore the system’s build, and every retraining is a rebuild.

A dataset then needs the disciplines code already has, namely immutable lineage, validation suites, and debugging when regressions appear. Code keeps its tests apart from what they test, and a dataset needs the same trust boundary between its splits. The training set is allowed to shape parameters; the validation set is allowed to shape modeling and pipeline choices; the test set is reserved for estimating generalization after those choices have been made. Data leakage occurs when information crosses those boundaries, so that an evaluation score rewards what the model or its designers already saw instead of measuring generalization to unseen data. Leakage enters through duplicate examples appearing in multiple splits, augmented variants of the same source record landing on both sides of the split, user records from the same household appearing in both training and test, or time-derived features computed using future observations (Kaufman et al. 2012). Preprocessing can leak too, a case section 4.7.1 takes up once normalization and encoding have been introduced.

Kaufman, Shachar, Saharon Rosset, Claudia Perlich, and Ori Stitelman. 2012. “Leakage in Data Mining: Formulation, Detection, and Avoidance.” ACM Transactions on Knowledge Discovery from Data (TKDD) 6 (4): 1–21.

Two of the book’s recurring lighthouse workloads (Lighthouse models put the iron law into practice) carry the chapter, one for each bound. Keyword spotting (KWS), the always-on wake-word detector behind phrases such as “Alexa” and “OK Google,” carries the question of what the data must contain. It takes over from the diabetic retinopathy screening case of ML Lifecycle because its data problems span both a cloud training pipeline and a microcontroller-class device. The model must recognize a few words across accents, microphones, and rooms, stay silent through months of ordinary speech, and fit in tens of kilobytes on a TinyML-class device. Its reference corpus, the Multilingual Spoken Words Corpus (Mazumder et al. 2021), is real; the smart-speaker requirements and data budget that this chapter sets for it are an illustrative scenario. ResNet-50 image classification on one accelerator carries the question of how fast data must arrive, because image examples are large enough to stress storage, decoding, and transfer in ways that one-second audio clips do not. KWS also shows the split boundary at work before any model exists. Assigning each speaker’s utterances to a single split keeps the acoustic model from memorizing speaker-specific vocal-tract resonances, so evaluation measures generalization to speakers the model has never heard.

Like a build, the pipeline that produces a dataset runs in stages, and the chapter follows them in the order of the two bounds. The capability half, which works the Data–Algorithm overlap, comes first. After the ways data fails and the requirements of the keyword spotter, acquisition, ingestion and validation, labeling, and processing build the dataset and settle what the model can learn, and a release step records exactly what was built. The throughput half works the Data–Machine overlap. The physics of moving datasets sets the cost of every byte, and storage and delivery must move those bytes fast enough to keep the accelerator fed. An acceptance test then checks both halves against the targets they started with, and operation keeps both bounds true after deployment.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team is building a training dataset from distributed sensor logs. They discover that the same event can appear in multiple log partitions. They add a pipeline step that identifies duplicate records and keeps one canonical copy. Where must this step run, and why?

    1. After splitting, because each split should be cleaned independently so that evaluation data is never touched by training-side processing.
    2. Only on the training split, because duplicates in validation and test data do not affect the trained model.
    3. Before splitting, because a duplicated record that lands on both sides of the split leaks the same evidence into evaluation and inflates the measured quality.
    4. Anywhere in the pipeline, because duplicates only waste storage and compute and do not change what the model learns.

  2. Explain why treating training data as the ‘source code’ of a machine learning system necessitates software-engineering practices such as immutable snapshot versioning, automated regression testing, and explicit split boundaries.

  3. True or False: In a keyword-spotting dataset, randomly partitioning audio recordings into an 80/20 train/test split at the individual audio file level maintains a valid trust boundary for estimating real-world generalization.

See Answers →

4.2 How Data Fails

Unlike a broken software build, which fails loudly at compile or test time, a broken data build often finishes cleanly and fails in production. A credit scoring model starts rejecting every applicant from one region after an upstream database migration silently changes how ZIP codes are typed; a wake-word detector misses more and more spoken commands for months after a device maker changes the microphone in its new models; a fraud detection system misses a new attack vector because its training data was six months stale. Each failure traces to a different root cause (schema drift, distribution shift, data staleness), yet all share a common pattern: a data defect entered early, every later stage treated the data as trustworthy, and the failure surfaced only after deployment. The four pillars framework of quality, reliability, scalability, and governance gives the checklist that catches them before they ship.

4.2.1 Data cascades

The pattern those three failures share is a data cascade, in which poor data quality in an early stage amplifies through every later stage of the pipeline (Sambasivan et al. 2021). Some invalid inputs trigger immediate software errors, but data can also pass schemas and tests while degrading learned behavior silently until quality issues become severe enough to require expensive investigation, rework, or retraining. In the lifecycle progression sequenced in figure 2, early defects in problem framing and collection bypass intermediate validation and surface only at evaluation and deployment.

↩︎ The compounding cost of late-stage data rework builds directly on the constraint propagation principle established in How Decisions Cascade: The Constraint Propagation Principle.

Definition 4.2: Data cascade

Data cascade is an ML systems failure mode in which upstream data quality problems propagate through collection, labeling, feature engineering, training, evaluation, and deployment, amplifying into downstream model failures or user harm.

  1. Significance: Remediation cost scales with the number of downstream artifacts that consumed the corrupted data: feature tables must be regenerated, models retrained, evaluation metrics recomputed, and deployed systems rolled back or patched, so one bad field can invalidate a whole training run and every experiment derived from it.
  2. Distinction: Unlike an isolated data quality bug, a data cascade is defined by propagation and amplification. Each pipeline stage treats its input as trustworthy and converts the defect into new derived state, which hides the root cause as the system moves farther from collection.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that ordinary software tests catch cascades. Corrupted data can satisfy schemas, pass unit tests, and produce successful training jobs while teaching the model the wrong signal, so prevention requires lineage, validation, contracts, and monitoring tied to model behavior.

Silence is what makes a cascade expensive. A software crash raises a stack trace and pages someone; a data-induced model error produces valid-looking outputs that are simply less accurate. By the time downstream metrics reflect the failure, corrupted predictions may already have contaminated historical data stores, forcing expensive rollbacks and data re-ingestion.

Example 4.1: The pipeline jungle
Scenario: The credit scoring model from the opening of this section suddenly begins rejecting all applicants from a specific geographic region.

Diagnosis: The training pipeline represented zip_code as a fixed-width string (“02139”). An upstream database migration changed the serving field to an integer without updating downstream data contracts. A downstream transform serialized that integer as “2139,” losing the leading zero and causing the model to treat a familiar postal code as an unobserved category. In this scenario, the fallback for unknown categories assigns high risk, explaining the regional rejection pattern.

Systems lesson: Unenforced feature boundaries create pipeline jungles (Sculley et al. 2015) where upstream schema changes cause silent downstream model failures. Versioned data contracts, which are machine-checked rules that declare expected types, value bounds, and whether nulls are allowed, stop cross-system corruption by rejecting malformed records at the ingestion boundary before they reach the model.

4.2.2 Four foundational pillars

The ZIP-code failure crossed several concerns at once: the values were wrong (quality), the transform broke silently instead of rejecting the record (reliability), and no contract recorded what the field meant (governance). Preventing failures like it means balancing four competing operational priorities (figure 3). Each feeds the ML data system, and each pulls against its neighbors: stricter validation costs throughput, stronger consistency limits distributed scale, privacy constraints cost performance, and bias mitigation can shrink the usable training set.

Quality governs input integrity and fitness for task: data must accurately reflect the operating distribution, with correct labels and uncorrupted features. In an edge voice-enrollment pipeline, for example, quality rules filter out clipped audio waveforms, low signal-to-noise ratios, and background acoustic interference so the model learns sharp decision boundaries rather than spurious acoustic artifacts.

Reliability asks whether that enrollment pipeline keeps working when the device, network, or user behavior is imperfect. A pipeline that produces excellent wake-word examples in a laboratory but fails during intermittent connectivity, battery pressure, or microphone glitches delivers no value in the user’s home. Error handling, retries, local buffering, and graceful degradation turn the quality rule into a usable system: the device can request another utterance, defer upload until connectivity returns, or preserve a known-good enrollment rather than silently accepting corrupted audio.

Scalability asks whether that same decision survives growth. A manual review policy that works for a thousand recordings collapses when the product expands to millions of users, dozens of languages, and long-tail acoustic conditions. The system must scale validation, storage, labeling, and retraining without letting infrastructure cost grow faster than the value of better coverage. The limiting resource depends on the workload. For edge speech, storage and labeling throughput dominate. For recommendation systems such as the DLRM lighthouse, the tables of learned user and item representations outgrow one machine’s memory and must be partitioned across several.

Governance defines the boundaries within which quality, reliability, and scalability must operate. For keyword spotting, governance determines whether raw voice recordings may leave the device, how long enrollment audio can be retained, which consent record authorizes use, and what documentation proves that the dataset covers relevant accents without exposing private speech. A perfectly scalable, reliable, high-quality pipeline that violates the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) or perpetuates demographic biases creates liability rather than value. Dataset documentation practices such as datasheets (Gebru et al. 2021) and data statements make part of that governance visible by recording provenance, intended use, collection conditions, and coverage needed for bias analysis and scientific comparison (Bender and Friedman 2018).

Bender, Emily M., and Batya Friedman. 2018. “Data Statements for Natural Language Processing: Toward Mitigating System Bias and Enabling Better Science.” Transactions of the Association for Computational Linguistics 6: 587–604. https://doi.org/10.1162/tacl_a_00041.

When an ML system fails, each class of symptom points to one of the four pillars:

  • Gradual accuracy loss points to quality. Drift has moved the serving distribution away from training, or label quality has fallen as annotator pools changed.
  • Intermittent pipeline failures point to reliability. Error handling, retry logic, or resource controls are missing under peak load.
  • Slow training on adequate hardware points to scalability. A single-threaded transformation, unpartitioned shuffle, or slow storage tier keeps parallel resources from doing useful work.
  • Compliance gaps found in audits point to governance debt. Lineage tracking is incomplete, access controls are stale, or retention policies have fallen behind regulatory changes.
Checkpoint 4.1: Four pillars framework

The four pillars provide a systems lens for every pipeline choice.

Pillars

Trade-offs

Some failures span multiple pillars. Features that differ between training and serving implicate both quality (the values are wrong) and reliability (the computation is inconsistent). A privacy-motivated deletion policy can also create quality gaps if the retained data no longer covers the deployment population. Diagnosing such cross-pillar failures requires checking consistency contracts, comparing feature distributions across environments, and tracing transformation lineage, so production diagnosis checks data infrastructure alongside model behavior. The pillars name what to check, but not how good is good enough. Those thresholds come from the requirements of a specific system, and the keyword spotter supplies them.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A data engineering team implements comprehensive synchronous schema and distribution validation checks directly inside the real-time event ingestion path. Under the Four Pillars framework, which primary operational trade-off will this team encounter?

    1. A Governance trade-off: inspecting payload schemas automatically breaches user data retention agreements.
    2. A Model Capacity trade-off: validating input records forces downstream neural network layers to increase parameter counts.
    3. A Scalability and Reliability trade-off: heavy synchronous validation consumes CPU cycles and increases per-record latency, reducing ingestion throughput and risking dropped messages during traffic spikes.
    4. A Durability trade-off: validating data records accelerates physical wear on persistent solid-state drive cells.

  2. True or False: Data cascades in ML systems are easily caught by standard software unit tests because corrupted input data causes deterministic assertion failures in pipeline code.

  3. Trace the propagation sequence of a data cascade as described in the chapter, from its root cause to user-facing impact:

  1. Downstream model optimization on distorted representations
  2. Upstream sensor or schema change without contract notification
  3. Silent distortion of extracted features passing syntactic checks
  4. Degraded real-world predictions and costly post-deployment rollback

See Answers →

4.3 Requirements and Data Budget

A wake-word detector answers to two numbers at once. It must catch nearly every spoken wake word, and it must almost never wake for anything else, while listening continuously in tens of kilobytes of memory on a microcontroller or the low-power core of a phone or smart speaker. The chapter’s illustrative smart speaker sets those numbers, and every later stage of the pipeline is measured against them.

In the wake-word interaction sequenced in figure 4, an always-on detector traps “Alexa…” before the smart speaker acknowledges the utterance and opens the command channel for “dim the lights.” Behind that brief exchange sits the data pipeline this chapter builds: the recordings the detector learned from, how they were labeled and checked, and how the result was verified before it shipped.

Turning that brief exchange into an engineering specification requires targets that reflect how the device actually listens. Overall accuracy is the wrong target, because almost every second of audio contains no wake word, and a detector that never fires would score nearly perfectly. The scenario therefore sets a keyword detection rate of 98 percent, the fraction of spoken wake words the detector catches, measured at a detection threshold that keeps false wakes within the budget the next notebook derives. It also requires a response within 200 ms and a model small and frugal enough for the device.

Napkin Math 4.1: False positive targets
Problem: An always-on KWS system budgets at most one expected false wake-up per month. Under a model that counts one nonkeyword classification window each second around the clock, how strict does the per-window false positive rate need to be?

Variables:

  • Duty cycle: Always-on (24 hours/day).
  • Window size: One-second classification windows.
  • Windows per month: One window per second, 24 hours/day, over 30 days gives 2,592,000 windows/month.

Math:

  • False positive rate (FPR): 1 expected false wake divided by the monthly nonkeyword-window count gives approximately \(3.9 \times 10^{-7}\)
  • Nonkeyword rejection requirement: 99.99996 percent rejection of nonkeyword windows.

Systems insight: This calculation bounds the expected monthly count, not the maximum number of false wakes in every month. Expectation does not require independent windows. Aggregate accuracy (for example, “99 percent accuracy”) is insufficient here. Evaluation must report false accepts per hour (FA/Hr) together with the corresponding false-rejection behavior. Real detectors score overlapping windows every few tens of milliseconds and smooth the scores, so the per-window target depends on the detector, while the per-hour rate is the quantity to measure. A budget of one false wake per month is deliberately strict; commercial assistants typically pass each candidate wake to a second, larger check rather than asking the always-on detector to meet such a budget alone (Siri Team 2017).

Siri Team. 2017. Hey Siri: An on-Device DNN-Powered Voice Trigger for Apple’s Personal Assistant.

Even 99.999 percent per-window rejection multiplies into 26 false wakes a month.

Nayak, Prateeth, Takuya Higuchi, Anmol Gupta, Shivesh Ranjan, Stephen Shum, Siddharth Sigtia, Erik Marchi, et al. 2022. “Improving Voice Trigger Detection with Metric Learning.” Interspeech 2022, 1896–900. https://doi.org/10.21437/interspeech.2022-11160.

Evaluation therefore reports the two rates together, as a detection/error trade-off curve that plots false accepts per hour against false rejection rate on streaming audio representative of deployment (Nayak et al. 2022). The detection target is read off that curve at the false-wake budget, and operational metrics complete the contract by bounding response time from the end of the keyword to the system’s reaction alongside the average power drawn while listening.

The device sets hard boundaries on any design that meets those targets. Memory limitations require lightweight models, often in the tens-of-kilobytes range (Zhang et al. 2017), to fit in the always-on island of the system-on-chip (SoC).1 This budget must accommodate both model weights and the feature extraction buffers and preprocessing state. Limited computational capabilities (often a few hundred MHz of clock speed) demand aggressive model optimization. On phones and wearables the detector runs from a battery, and even a plugged-in smart speaker keeps its always-on stage to milliwatts so that idle power stays low, so KWS systems target sub-milliwatt consumption during continuous listening.

Zhang, Yundong, Naveen Suda, Liangzhen Lai, and Vikas Chandra. 2017. Hello Edge: Keyword Spotting on Microcontrollers.

1 SoC always-on island: Modern system-on-chip designs partition power domains so a low-power “always-on” island (typically achieving sub-milliwatt draw) monitors for wake triggers while the main processor sleeps. The critical constraint is that this island must hold both the model weights and the audio preprocessing code within its dedicated SRAM, a split budget that forces KWS architectures to optimize for total footprint, not just parameter count.

Data quality and diversity determine whether these targets can be met inside those limits. The dataset must cover speakers of different accents, ages, and genders, the many ways people pronounce the same wake word, and background conditions from quiet bedrooms to noisy kitchens. The model cannot grow to compensate for missing coverage, so on this device the data carries the burden that a larger model would carry in a data center.

4.3.1 KWS design space

Doubling the sampling rate of a wake-word recording from 8 kHz to 16 kHz captures sharper consonant sounds and raises the detection rate, but it also doubles the raw-audio window and the input-processing work on the device. Every data engineering choice for the keyword spotter pulls detection rate, false-wake tolerance, latency, energy, and memory against one another, and table 1 prices those trade-offs. Each row is a D·A·M coupling of the kind drawn in The D·A·M Axis Triad, because a data choice such as sampling rate or coefficient count changes what the algorithm receives and what the machine must hold in always-on memory. One row uses mel-frequency cepstral coefficients (MFCCs)2 (Davis and Mermelstein 1980), which reduce each frame of roughly 25 ms of audio to 13 to 40 numbers that emphasize the frequencies speech uses. The coefficient count sets the size of the model’s input and how much acoustic detail it keeps; section 4.7 shows how they are extracted.

2 MFCC (mel-frequency cepstral coefficients): Mel-scale filtering gives this representation its compactness and noise resistance by emphasizing the frequencies humans use to distinguish speech. It reduces the hundreds of raw samples in a short window (400 samples in 25 ms at 16 kHz) to 13–40 coefficients, so each frame fits inside an always-on, kilobyte-scale memory budget. A parameter mismatch between training and serving creates feature skew and can degrade accuracy.

Davis, Steven B., and Paul Mermelstein. 1980. “Comparison of Parametric Representations for Monosyllabic Word Recognition in Continuously Spoken Sentences.” IEEE Transactions on Acoustics, Speech, and Signal Processing 28 (4): 357–66. https://doi.org/10.1109/TASSP.1980.1163420.

Because the device runs inside a milliwatt power envelope and a tens-of-kilobytes SRAM budget, an engineering team cannot pick the most accurate option in every row of table 1 at once. Instead, the team must divide a fixed data budget across labeling, storage, and governance so the detector reaches its accuracy target without outgrowing the hardware. Notebook 4.2 allocates the data budget and proposes a candidate design for a smart-speaker deployment. The acquisition, labeling, processing, and delivery decisions that follow supply the evidence needed to accept or reject it.

Napkin Math 4.2: Allocating the KWS data budget
Scenario: A KWS system for a smart speaker has these constraints:

  • Target: 98 percent keyword detection rate at a threshold that keeps the expected false-wake budget below 1 false wake/month
  • Budget: $150K total data engineering budget
  • Memory: 256 KB model size limit (always-on island), with a separate 16 KB budget for preprocessing state
  • Timeline: 6 months to production

Step 1: Choose conservative options for the constrained device.

For this scenario, the team chooses two conservative options:

  • 13 MFCC coefficients, because every MFCC frame lives in the always-on island’s SRAM as model input and buffered state, so the 40-coefficient option triples a cost the device pays continuously
  • Local inference, because streaming every second of audio to a server is ruled out by bandwidth, privacy, and offline operation

The choice rests on where each cost lands. Data collection is paid once, from a central budget, whereas the 40-coefficient representation triples the model’s input, and with it the first layer’s work and the feature buffer, on every device for every second it listens. The table suggests a 3–5 percent detection gain from 40 coefficients, so the team picks 13 and spends the difference on coverage, expecting broader training data to recover the gap. The acceptance test of section 4.11 decides the choice.

Step 2: Calculate budget allocation.

Labeling is priced at a scenario rate per crowdsourced example, set above the image-classification crowd rates that section 4.6.2 later tabulates because a KWS contributor must record the utterance as well as label it. The $150K budget splits across three cost categories:

  • Labeling (~60 percent): $90K available
  • Storage/Processing (~25 percent): $37.5K
  • Governance/Other (~15 percent): $22.5K

At $0.10/label with 20 percent review overhead: $90K ÷ $0.12/label = 750K labeled examples

This yields roughly 0.75M labeled examples, just below the 1M anchor in the design space. The 1M-to-10M row in table 1 should therefore be read as a scaling reference rather than a direct interpolation: the real-label budget alone does not buy the full data-volume gain. Counting examples also hides what limits a wake-word model in practice, which is the number of distinct speakers and the hours of ordinary, non-keyword audio available to measure false wakes; section 4.11 prices that listening time.

Step 3: Identify candidate quality improvements.

The quality plan has three components:

  • An illustrative base-model detection rate of ~90 percent
  • Partial data-volume gain from 750K real examples
  • A gap to the 98 percent target that the options below must close

Three options from the design space are candidates for closing the gap; their collection, storage, processing, and deployed-footprint costs must be checked against the allocations. Their listed gains cannot simply be added to the base detection rate, because they overlap (noisy data and synthetic augmentation both buy robustness to acoustic conditions). The plan is therefore a candidate whose combined effect must be measured on the trained system:

  • 16 kHz sampling: a detection-rate gain of 2–4 percent, 2× raw-audio storage (requires cost validation; on the device only the short window of raw samples feeding each MFCC computation doubles, because every frame still reduces to 13 coefficients)
  • Noisy training data: a field detection-rate gain of 10–15 percent, 3× collection cost (proposed for a targeted noisy slice; its cost must fit the storage/processing allocation)
  • Synthetic augmentation: a robustness gain of 3–5 percent, 10× cheaper than real data in the illustrative row; generation, verification, and storage costs remain to be measured

Step 4: Final configuration.

The scenario combines these choices with 13 MFCCs and the computed 750K real-label budget in table 2. The quality effects can overlap, so the table records a candidate configuration rather than a solved optimum.

Result: The budget buys approximately 750K human-labeled examples, 13 MFCCs, local inference, and a noisy slice plus synthetic augmentation to close the quality gap. Whether that configuration reaches the 98 percent detection target inside the 256 KB model limit is what acceptance decides.

Systems insight: Design-space analysis turns “we need more data” into a costed plan. The stated labeling assumptions buy about 750K real examples, and the acceptance test, not the design table, decides whether that plan succeeds.

Table 2 records the resulting configuration, pairing each design choice with the constraint that motivates it: sampling and augmentation draw on the stated allocations, while the always-on island sets the deployed-footprint constraint. Actual costs and working-state memory still require validation.

The candidate configuration fixes the targets but not the data. Meeting them starts with acquisition: finding recordings that cover the speakers, accents, devices, and rooms the detector will meet, from sources whose cost and consent terms the budget can bear.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An always-on Keyword Spotting (KWS) system on an embedded voice assistant continuously evaluates 1-second audio classification windows (\(24\text{ hours/day}\) over a \(30\text{-day}\) month). Treat each window as nonkeyword audio. The product budgets at most 1 expected false activation per month. An engineer reports 99% aggregate accuracy and, separately, a measured 1% false positive rate on background noise. How many false activations does that FPR imply in expectation, and what per-window FPR meets the expected-count budget?

    1. A 1% FPR implies an expected count of 720 false activations per month; the expected-count budget requires a per-window FPR of \(\le 1.38 \times 10^{-5}\).
    2. A 1% FPR implies an expected count of ~25,920 false activations per month (~36 false wakes/hour); the expected-count budget requires a per-window FPR of \(\le 3.86 \times 10^{-7}\) (>99.9999% non-keyword rejection).
    3. A 1% FPR implies an expected count of ~2,592 false activations per month; the expected-count budget requires a per-window FPR of \(\le 1.0 \times 10^{-4}\).
    4. A 1% FPR satisfies the expected-count budget because accuracy is averaged over the total number of audio hours across the entire device fleet.

  2. The chapter specifies its keyword spotter by a keyword detection rate measured at a threshold that holds the expected false-wake budget, rather than by overall accuracy. Why is overall accuracy the wrong target for an always-on detector?

    1. Almost every one-second window contains no wake word, so a detector that never fires scores near-perfect overall accuracy while missing every command; the meaningful targets are the fraction of spoken wake words caught and the false-wake rate at the chosen threshold.
    2. Overall accuracy cannot be computed for a model that runs on a microcontroller, so the target must use a metric the device can calculate itself.
    3. Overall accuracy rises automatically with more training data, so it cannot distinguish one data plan from another.
    4. Detection rate and overall accuracy are the same quantity for a binary classifier; the scenario only renames it.

See Answers →

4.4 Data Acquisition

A huge corpus of studio audio does not help a wake-word detector that has to run on inexpensive microphones in noisy kitchens, because a model cannot learn a speaker, room, or microphone that no source supplied. Data acquisition therefore sets the outer limit of the capability bound, and it begins when the team names the coverage gap the model must close. For keyword spotting, public corpora are the first source. Speech Commands (Warden 2018) established the small-vocabulary benchmark of one-second spoken words, and the Multilingual Spoken Words Corpus (MSWC) (Mazumder et al. 2021) provides a reusable reference source of 23.4 million audio samples spanning 50 languages. That corpus is larger than the product-specific labeled subset budgeted in the KWS design example; using it still requires selecting and validating relevant coverage.

Warden, Pete. 2018. “Speech Commands: A Dataset for Limited-Vocabulary Speech Recognition.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1804.03209, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1804.03209.
Mazumder, Mark, Sharad Chitlangia, Colby Banbury, Yiping Kang, Juan Manuel Ciro, Keith Achorn, Daniel Galvez, et al. 2021. “Multilingual Spoken Words Corpus.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 35 (NeurIPS 2021) Datasets and Benchmarks Track.

Reaching a 98 percent detection rate requires representative coverage of regional accents, speaker age groups, room reverbs, and microphones that differ in frequency response, signal-to-noise ratio, and clipping behavior, at volumes that manual curation cannot economically supply and under strict privacy boundaries on audio retention. Because no single collection method can economically cover all of those conditions, acquisition strategy is a sequence of gap-closing decisions: reuse what already matches deployment, collect what is missing, scrape or synthesize where scale is the binding constraint, and reject sources whose provenance or consent constraints make them unusable.

4.4.1 Coverage and diversity requirements

Making those gap-closing decisions requires looking past aggregate scores, because a wake-word detector trained on millions of clean clips can score well on an overall validation set while still waking once an hour on kitchen conversation or missing speakers with underrepresented regional accents. Dataset scale alone does not guarantee generalization when coverage gaps in a large corpus (geographic bias, demographic underrepresentation, sensor calibration drift, and unobserved edge cases) cause systematic failures that aggregate validation metrics hide (Shankar et al. 2017; Wang et al. 2019; Oakden-Rayner et al. 2020).

Shankar, Shreya, Yoni Halpern, Eric Breck, James Atwood, Jimbo Wilson, and David Sculley. 2017. “No Classification Without Representation: Assessing Geodiversity Issues in Open Data Sets for the Developing World.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1711.08536.
Wang, Tianlu, Jieyu Zhao, Mark Yatskar, Kai-Wei Chang, and Vicente Ordonez. 2019. “Balanced Datasets Are Not Enough: Estimating and Mitigating Gender Bias in Deep Image Representations.” 2019 IEEE/CVF International Conference on Computer Vision (ICCV), 5309–18. https://doi.org/10.1109/iccv.2019.00541.
Oakden-Rayner, Luke, Jared Dunnmon, Gustavo Carneiro, and Christopher Re. 2020. “Hidden Stratification Causes Clinically Meaningful Failures in Machine Learning for Medical Imaging.” Proceedings of the ACM Conference on Health, Inference, and Learning, 151–59. https://doi.org/10.1145/3368555.3384468.

For KWS, coverage must support both keyword recognition and the false-wake contract. Labeled wake-word clips measure recognition, but acquiring more of them does not establish how often an always-on detector activates on ordinary nonkeyword audio. The acquisition plan must separately reserve consented recordings collected for an evaluation study of representative listening conditions, including background conversation, television, music, silence, and environmental noise across the intended microphones and rooms. These recordings retain their duration and temporal sequence so evaluation can run the deployed detection path and report false accepts per hour alongside false rejection on keyword recordings. A short stream with no observed false wakes supplies limited evidence; its duration and the uncertainty of the measured rate constrain any claim about the monthly target.

Training negatives serve a different acquisition goal. Mining near-wake phrases and other audio that confuses the current detector can improve its rejection behavior, but this deliberately difficult sample does not preserve the frequency of those conditions in deployment. The representative evaluation streams therefore remain separate from training and threshold selection, with speaker and recording-period boundaries maintained. An acquisition budget that funds only keyword labels and mined hard negatives can improve the detector while leaving its operational false-wake target unmeasured.

Governance constraints further shape acquisition: privacy and health-data regulations such as GDPR and the U.S. Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA) limit what data can be collected and how (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016; United States Congress 1996), while ethical sourcing requires fair compensation and transparent use of human contributions. Together, these coverage, evaluation, and governance bounds define the criteria against which each candidate data source is judged.

European Parliament, and Council of the European Union. 2016. Regulation (EU) 2016/679 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 27 April 2016. Official Journal of the European Union.
United States Congress. 1996. Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act of 1996. Public Law 104-191.

↪ The governance infrastructure that proves what data a production system ingested, who accessed it, and on what legal basis is developed in Data Governance and Compliance.

4.4.2 Selecting data sources

Paying for custom voice recordings of words and accents that a public benchmark already covers wastes the data budget, while stopping at the benchmark leaves the detector blind to its own microphones and rooms. The choice among curated datasets, expert crowdsourcing, controlled web scraping, and synthetic generation therefore begins with the cheapest reusable source and escalates to active collection or simulation only when the remaining deployment-distribution gap justifies the added cost, quality effort, and governance risk.

Preexisting datasets from repositories such as Kaggle, UCI (Dua and Graff 2024), and ImageNet3 are the first test. They offer speed and comparability when the deployment distribution resembles the benchmark enough to make reuse meaningful. For KWS, a curated speech corpus can establish the baseline model and reveal which words, languages, and acoustic conditions are already covered. A baseline corpus does not guarantee operational coverage, but it makes the remaining distribution gaps measurable.

Dua, Dheeru, and Casey Graff. 2024. UCI Machine Learning Repository.

3 ImageNet: The 2009 paper reported 3.2 million images across 5,247 synsets; the full database later reached about 14.2 million images across 21,841 synsets, whereas the challenge used a 1,000-class subset (Deng et al. 2009, 2024; Russakovsky et al. 2015). Its value as a benchmark is inseparable from its data engineering: Fei-Fei Li’s team built labeling infrastructure that later teams could reuse. The catch is sensitivity to annotation procedures and modest distribution shifts: models tuned to ImageNet’s distribution can underperform on related but shifted test sets (Recht et al. 2019; Beyer et al. 2020).

Deng, Jia, Wei Dong, Richard Socher, Li-Jia Li, Kai Li, and Li Fei-Fei. 2009. “ImageNet: A Large-Scale Hierarchical Image Database.” 2009 IEEE Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition, 248–55. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2009.5206848.
Deng, Jia, Wei Dong, Richard Socher, Li-Jia Li, Kai Li, and Li Fei-Fei. 2024. ImageNet.
Russakovsky, Olga, Jia Deng, Hao Su, Jonathan Krause, Sanjeev Satheesh, Sean Ma, Zhiheng Huang, et al. 2015. “ImageNet Large Scale Visual Recognition Challenge.” International Journal of Computer Vision 115 (3): 211–52. https://doi.org/10.1007/s11263-015-0816-y.
Recht, Benjamin, Rebecca Roelofs, Ludwig Schmidt, and Vaishaal Shankar. 2019. “Do ImageNet Classifiers Generalize to ImageNet?” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 5389–400.
Beyer, Lucas, Olivier J. Hénaff, Alexander Kolesnikov, Xiaohua Zhai, and Aäron van den Oord. 2020. “Are We Done with ImageNet?” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2006.07159.
Pineau, Joelle, Philippe Vincent-Lamarre, Koustuv Sinha, Vincent Larivière, Alina Beygelzimer, Florence d’Alché-Buc, Emily Fox, and Hugo Larochelle. 2021. “Improving Reproducibility in Machine Learning Research (a Report from the Neurips 2019 Reproducibility Program).” Journal of Machine Learning Research 22 (164): 1–20.
Henderson, Peter, Riashat Islam, Philip Bachman, Joelle Pineau, Doina Precup, and David Meger. 2018. “Deep Reinforcement Learning That Matters.” Proceedings of the AAAI Conference on Artificial Intelligence 32 (1): 3207–14. https://doi.org/10.1609/aaai.v32i1.11694.
Gudivada, Venkat N., Amy Apon, and Junhua Ding. 2017. “Data Quality Considerations for Big Data and Machine Learning: Going Beyond Data Cleaning and Transformations.” International Journal on Advances in Software 10 (1–2): 1–20.

Whether an external dataset is safe to reuse depends on how well it is documented. Without records of sensor calibration, recording environments, and filtering rules, a team cannot reproduce an external dataset’s conditions or check whether they match deployment (Pineau et al. 2021; Henderson et al. 2018). Good documentation states how the data was collected, how each label was defined, and what baseline error rates were found, so another team can validate the corpus independently. Once ingestion reaches millions of examples from varied sources, hidden label errors also multiply past what humans can inspect by hand (Gudivada et al. 2017), so the pipeline must run automated validation checks on every batch.

Reuse carries a less visible risk. Standard validation metrics can create an illusion of model readiness when the benchmark itself is unrepresentative, and when many teams train on the same repository, its blind spots do not average out across their models. They can recur across models, so five models trained on one dataset can share correlated failures (figure 5).

This gap between benchmark and deployment is the central failure mode of static offline evaluation. Because training minimizes average error only over the observed training set (empirical risk minimization), a model will latch onto whatever shortcuts appear in a static benchmark, even when those shortcuts vanish in production. Closing the remaining gaps requires acquiring new data beyond the benchmark, and doing so across millions of examples turns source selection into a scaling problem.

4.4.3 Scaling acquisition

Curated repositories and small-batch recording studies close coverage gaps one slice at a time, which stops working once a task needs millions of training instances. At large scale, web scraping, crowdsourcing, and synthetic generation provide the main routes to very large datasets. Data-acquisition scalability is an economic problem: unit labeling costs, ingestion throughput, and storage overhead scale along very different curves, and labeling usually dominates (section 4.6.2). Approaches that work well at thousands of examples become impractical at millions, whereas automated collection, distributed human annotation, and algorithmic generation scale along different cost and quality frontiers.

Web scraping reduces the cost of collecting raw examples at scale. Supervision remains a separate expense unless the source and task already supply usable labels or training targets. Foundational vision datasets such as ImageNet (Deng et al. 2024) and OpenImages (Kuznetsova et al. 2020) relied on automated scraping, while modern language models depend on multi-terabyte web crawls (Groeneveld et al. 2024). Ingestion pipelines targeting domain-specific corpora, such as public code repositories (Chen et al. 2021), expand coverage at low per-sample acquisition costs. Scrapers are also fragile: page layouts change and break parsers, sites throttle crawlers, and dynamically rendered content goes missing. Raw web crawls also capture anachronistic or irrelevant samples that contaminate supervised targets.

Kuznetsova, Alina, Hassan Rom, Neil Alldrin, Jasper Uijlings, Ivan Krasin, Jordi Pont-Tuset, Shahab Kamali, et al. 2020. “The Open Images Dataset V4: Unified Image Classification, Object Detection, and Visual Relationship Detection at Scale.” International Journal of Computer Vision 128 (7): 1956–81. https://doi.org/10.1007/s11263-020-01316-z.
Groeneveld, Dirk, Iz Beltagy, Pete Walsh, Akshita Bhagia, Rodney Kinney, Oyvind Tafjord, Ananya Harsh Jha, et al. 2024. “OLMo: Accelerating the Science of Language Models.” Proceedings of the 62nd Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 15789–809. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2024.acl-long.841.
Chen, Mark, Jerry Tworek, Heewoo Jun, Qiming Yuan, Henrique Ponde de Oliveira Pinto, Jared Kaplan, Harri Edwards, et al. 2021. “Evaluating Large Language Models Trained on Code.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2107.03374.

The 1914 street scene in figure 6 captures this failure mode directly. An uncurated web query for “traffic light” returned a historical Cleveland intersection where streetcars and a roadside control booth surround early manually switched signals. If an autonomous driving model trained on such samples, it would learn spurious correlations with trolley tracks, period attire, and handwritten label annotations rather than modern LED traffic lights.

Raw volume cannot substitute for checking what the examples mean, because an uncurated web crawl multiplies spurious correlations along with sample count. Scraping also operates under strict legal and governance boundaries. Website terms of service, robots.txt directives, and evolving copyright law limit what automated crawlers may take (Harvard Law School 2024). Ingestion pipelines must therefore record provenance, filter for copyright compliance, and scrub personal data before scraped files enter shared storage.

Harvard Law School. 2024. Does ChatGPT Violate New York Times Copyrights?

Even a well-governed crawl draws on a finite supply. Within the post-2010 climb of figure 1, pretraining corpora for large language models grew at a fitted rate of about 2.9× per year, from the 300 billion tokens that trained GPT-3 in 2020 to the 36 trillion reported for Qwen3 in 2025 (Brown et al. 2020; Yang et al. 2025). Figure 7 sets that climb against the estimated stock of public human-generated text, roughly 300 trillion tokens once quality and repetition are accounted for (Villalobos et al. 2024), and at the fitted rate the largest corpora reach the middle of that estimate around 2028. Once quality text runs short, acquiring more of it stops being a purchasing decision, and filtering, deduplication, and the selection methods of Data Selection decide how much each additional token is worth. A wake-word model meets its own version of the limit far sooner. No crawl holds enough clean recordings of one phrase in every accent and acoustic setting, so the KWS pipeline has to commission the data it lacks.

Crowdsourcing shifts the acquisition bottleneck from raw sample retrieval to quality control across distributed human annotators. Platforms like Amazon Mechanical Turk4 (Amazon Web Services 2024) demonstrated this at scale with ImageNet, distributing millions of image categorization tasks across thousands of independent contributors (Deng et al. 2024). Crowdsourcing provides two core systems advantages: horizontal scaling through parallel microtask dispatch, and demographic diversity across regional accents and acoustic settings. However, distributed human labeling introduces significant variance in label fidelity. To keep label noise from corrupting what the model learns, the acquisition pipeline must implement automated quality control: consensus voting, golden-set validation (interleaving pre-labeled control samples), and dynamic task routing (Sheng and Zhang 2019). In the KWS pipeline, crowdsourced collection targets wake-word samples from specific acoustic environments and underrepresented dialects that public web scrapes fail to capture.

4 Amazon Mechanical Turk (MTurk): A crowdsourcing platform that routes small tasks to distributed workers and can scale annotation beyond expert-only workflows. Snow et al. (2008) evaluate this pattern for natural-language annotation tasks, showing that non-expert annotations can be useful when task design and quality control are handled carefully. For wake-word audio collection, the same systems trade-off appears in domain-specific form: scale is attractive, but submissions still need acoustic checks such as signal-to-noise ratio, duration, and recording validity before they can safely enter the training set.

Snow, Rion, Brendan O’Connor, Daniel Jurafsky, and Andrew Y. Ng. 2008. “Cheap and Fast—but Is It Good? Evaluating Non-Expert Annotations for Natural Language Tasks.” Proceedings of the Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing - EMNLP ’08, 254. https://doi.org/10.3115/1613715.1613751.
Amazon Web Services. 2024. Amazon Mechanical Turk.
Sheng, Victor S., and Jing Zhang. 2019. “Machine Learning with Crowdsourcing: A Brief Summary of the Past Research and Future Directions.” Proceedings of the AAAI Conference on Artificial Intelligence 33 (01): 9837–43. https://doi.org/10.1609/aaai.v33i01.33019837.

Synthetic data generation reduces physical collection and labeling labor by generating training samples algorithmically. Generation still incurs per-sample compute and storage costs, alongside verification costs. Its learning value depends on whether the generator represents task-relevant conditions in the target deployment environment. In practice, simulation outputs and real-world historical telemetry flow along two tracks into a blending stage that feeds model training (figure 8).

↪ Synthetic data as a lever on what the model learns, from augmentation of real samples to generative synthesis and closing the domain gap, is developed in Synthetic Data Generation.

Synthetic data is especially useful for rare events and controlled augmentation. Physics simulators can generate tail cases, such as high-speed sensor saturation or near-collision trajectories, that are too dangerous or costly to record in the field (NVIDIA 2024). In vision, augmentation pipelines apply learned or random combinations of spatial and color distortions (Cubuk et al. 2019, 2020; Shorten and Khoshgoftaar 2019). In speech processing, SpecAugment warps time and masks frequency bands directly on the spectrogram, forcing the acoustic model to stop relying on any single narrow frequency band (Park et al. 2019). For the KWS system, parametric text-to-speech synthesis (Werchniak et al. 2021) produces clean wake-word utterances that the pipeline then convolves with room impulse responses (RIRs). Because a room impulse response captures the direct sound and wall reflections produced by a brief acoustic pulse in a specific space, blending the clean waveform with that response makes the utterance sound as if it were spoken in rooms with varied reverberation times before synthetic background noise is added.

NVIDIA. 2024. NVIDIA Omniverse and Simulation.
Cubuk, Ekin D., Barret Zoph, Dandelion Mané, Vijay Vasudevan, and Quoc V. Le. 2019. “AutoAugment: Learning Augmentation Strategies from Data.” 2019 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 113–23. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2019.00020.
Cubuk, Ekin D., Barret Zoph, Jonathon Shlens, and Quoc V. Le. 2020. “Randaugment: Practical Automated Data Augmentation with a Reduced Search Space.” 2020 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition Workshops (CVPRW), 3008–17. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvprw50498.2020.00359.
Shorten, Connor, and Taghi M. Khoshgoftaar. 2019. “A Survey on Image Data Augmentation for Deep Learning.” Journal of Big Data 6 (1): 1–48. https://doi.org/10.1186/s40537-019-0197-0.
Park, Daniel S., William Chan, Yu Zhang, Chung-Cheng Chiu, Barret Zoph, Ekin D. Cubuk, and Quoc V. Le. 2019. “SpecAugment: A Simple Data Augmentation Method for Automatic Speech Recognition.” Interspeech 2019, 2613–17. https://doi.org/10.21437/interspeech.2019-2680.
Werchniak, Andrew, Roberto Barra Chicote, Yuriy Mishchenko, Jasha Droppo, Jeff Condal, Peng Liu, and Anish Shah. 2021. “Exploring the Application of Synthetic Audio in Training Keyword Spotters.” ICASSP 2021 - 2021 IEEE International Conference on Acoustics, Speech and Signal Processing (ICASSP), 7993–96. https://doi.org/10.1109/icassp39728.2021.9413448.

Example 4.2: Synthetic data generation
Scenario: A keyword-spotting team lacks sufficient physical recordings of rare speaker accents, room acoustics, and background noise profiles.

Diagnosis: Pure physical collection cannot cover rare acoustic edge cases within project deadlines. Generating synthetic audio variations via pitch shifting, additive noise, and room impulse response simulation expands training set diversity without expensive field collection.

Systems lesson: Augmentation is cheap, but it does not prove the model works on real accents. Targeted augmentation proposes expanded acoustic coverage; pitch, noise, and room-response changes do not establish fidelity to rare accents. Held-out real recordings across the relevant speaker and acoustic conditions must establish whether the expansion improves deployment quality.

Starting from the reference corpus, the team closes the product’s remaining coverage gaps by matching each source to what it does best per dollar. Public benchmarks seed the baseline model at low cost; web scraping supplies bulk acoustic variety; crowdsourcing collects targeted recordings for underrepresented dialects; and simulation fills in rare acoustic distortions. Together these sources form a candidate coverage plan. Held-out real recordings then test which gaps that plan actually closes, while measured generation, collection, labeling, and storage costs decide whether it fits the budget; the deployed pipeline still requires footprint validation.

Acquisition ends with data that covers the deployment on paper and arrives in many shapes: crowdsourced recordings, synthetic waveforms, and web audio with different encodings, sampling rates, and noise floors. None of it has yet been checked. Before any record can be labeled or trained on, it must pass a boundary that admits only well-formed, trustworthy data.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Multiple independent autonomous driving teams train their perception models exclusively on a popular public driving benchmark. What systemic failure mode does this practice introduce into the broader ecosystem?

    1. Shared dataset bias propagation, where common blind spots, annotation artifacts, and unrepresented edge cases can create correlated weaknesses across deployed models.
    2. Catastrophic memory leaks in GPU driver kernels caused by repeated reading of shared image formats.
    3. Immediate violation of data gravity constraints due to distributed multi-tenant reads.
    4. Automatic over-fitting to hardware memory hierarchies during distributed gradient synchronization.

  2. Discuss the primary advantages and critical risks of using synthetic data generation (e.g., 3D graphics rendering or generative audio simulation) as a core data acquisition strategy.

  3. True or False: Achieving state-of-the-art benchmark accuracy on a curated dataset (such as ImageNet or Common Voice) guarantees that an ML model is ready for deployment in real-world production environments.

  4. Order the following gap-closing acquisition steps into a sensible workflow: (1) choose an acquisition source for the remaining gap under budget and governance constraints, (2) define the coverage gap the model must close, (3) validate that the acquired data actually improves the missing coverage and meets quality requirements, (4) audit what existing sources and provenance already provide.

See Answers →

4.5 Ingestion and Validation

A crowdsourced wake-word recording arrives as a stereo MP3, a synthetic one as a mono WAV file at a different sampling rate, and a clip from a consenting user’s device as a compressed fragment stamped in the wrong time zone. The ingestion boundary turns this mix into one consistent record format and decides which records may enter at all. It is the front end of the data pipeline in figure 9, where records pass through ingestion and central storage before the transformation loops that feed training, inside a governance perimeter that controls what may be kept and who may read it. For keyword spotting, the boundary handles the recorded examples used to build and update the model; the detector’s live audio on the device never passes through it.

The tiers in figure 9 keep raw ingestion separate from the validation, transformation, and feature-engineering loops that feed training, so a slow transformation never stops records from arriving, and a check added at ingestion protects every stage after it. Two decisions shape that boundary before any record is checked: when records are taken in, and where they are transformed relative to storage.

4.5.1 Batch vs. streaming ingestion patterns

The first decision, when records are taken in, depends on how quickly data loses value and how much infrastructure cost that freshness justifies. A fraud detector that flags a stolen card hours after the purchase arrives after the money has moved, whereas a week’s worth of crowdsourced wake-word recordings loses nothing by waiting for the next scheduled training run. Batch systems gain efficiency by tolerating that staleness, while streaming systems gain freshness by taking on continuous operational complexity.

Batch ingestion collects records over a period and processes them together on a schedule. It amortizes startup cost across large volumes, runs when compute is cheapest, and releases its resources between runs, which suits data whose value decays slowly (Akidau et al. 2015), such as the new crowdsourced recordings and opt-in diagnostic clips that the keyword spotter folds into its next training run. Batch processing also simplifies recovery and reproducibility: a failed batch can be rerun from its inputs, inspected, and reprocessed after a fix, and the result is repeatable when inputs, code, configuration, and randomness are controlled.

Akidau, Tyler, Robert Bradshaw, Craig Chambers, Slava Chernyak, Rafael J. Fernández-Moctezuma, Reuven Lax, Sam McVeety, et al. 2015. “The Dataflow Model: A Practical Approach to Balancing Correctness, Latency, and Cost in Massive-Scale, Unbounded, Out-of-Order Data Processing.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 8 (12): 1792–803. https://doi.org/10.14778/2824032.2824076.

Stream ingestion processes each record as it arrives, which is necessary whenever data loses value within seconds, as in real-time fraud scoring. However, stream processing introduces complexity that batch processing avoids. The system must handle backpressure, the condition where downstream systems cannot keep pace with incoming data rates. During traffic spikes, when a sudden surge produces data faster than processing capacity, the system must either buffer data (requiring memory and introducing latency), sample (losing some data), or push back to producers (potentially causing their failures). Late or out-of-order events further complicate windowed aggregations, because the system must hold per-window state and track event time rather than arrival time. These requirements raise compute and memory overhead, so freshness guarantees trade directly against infrastructure cost and operational burden. Data freshness service level agreements (SLAs) specify maximum acceptable delays between data generation and processing. Meeting a 100-millisecond SLA requires different infrastructure than meeting a one-hour SLA, affecting networking, storage, and processing architectures.

Many production ML systems combine the two. A recommendation system might use streaming ingestion for real-time user interactions to update session-based recommendations immediately, while using batch ingestion for overnight processing of user profiles and item features; Notebook 4.3 quantifies the financial trade-off between these two operational regimes.

Napkin Math 4.3: The cost of real-time
Problem: A pipeline ingests 1M events/s. Which is cheaper, Batch (hourly) or Stream (sub-second) ingestion?

Physics:

  1. Throughput: At 1M events/s and 1 KB per event, the pipeline carries 1 GB/s.
  2. Stream requirements: To sustain 1 GB/s with less than 100 ms latency, the design assumes each core can parse, validate, and update features for about 20 MB/s of events, which takes 50 primary cores. A standby core for each provisions recovery capacity, for 100 always-on cores in total. That capacity does not establish the 100 ms target: failure detection, state recovery, replay, and the full ingestion path must also be validated under failure. Running those cores for 24 hours/day at $0.05/hr costs $120/day.
  3. Batch requirements: Process 3.6 TB (1-hour window) in 10 minutes. That is 6 GB/s in aggregate. Sequential reads over large files let each core sustain about 30 MB/s, more than a streaming core handling events one at a time. The batch design therefore needs 200 cores for 10 minutes per clock hour, accumulating 800 core-hours per day. At $0.05/hr, that costs $40/day.

Systems insight: Under these provisioning and per-core throughput assumptions, streaming compute costs approximately 3× as much as batch compute for the same data volume. This premium is justified when the value of subsecond latency exceeds the added cost.

The premium comes from this scenario’s provisioning assumptions and leaves out storage, buffering, and failover, so the choice still rests on the feature freshness that inference actually needs. The ingestion paradigm also shapes how the downstream model trains. Batch ingestion naturally pairs with periodic retraining: data accumulates in storage, an automated job retrains the model on the updated corpus, and a verified checkpoint deploys to serving. Stream ingestion makes that batch boundary less natural, raising the possibility of continuous online learning, in which model weights update directly as events arrive. Continuous updates, however, introduce operational risks that batch checkpoints avoid. Sudden distribution shifts, corrupted records, or adversarial payloads from any public-facing input path can reach the model before validation gates intervene. Most production systems therefore decouple ingestion from training: they stream data into a durable buffer while training on validated batches, reserving continuous online updates for tightly bounded, low-risk domains.

War Story 4.1: When users became the training set (2016)
Context: In March 2016, Microsoft launched Tay, a Twitter chatbot designed to engage with users online and adapt its responses from public interactions (Lee 2016).

Mechanism: Adversarial users coordinated toxic prompts and exploited a public interaction surface, including behavior that repeated user-supplied text.

Impact: Tay began tweeting abusive, racist, and misogynistic statements within 16 hours of deployment.

Fix: Microsoft suspended the chatbot 16 hours after launch, apologized publicly, and stated that a relaunch would require stronger safeguards against misuse.

Systems lesson: Public ingestion paths require adversarial-input controls. Otherwise, a feature that accepts user-generated content also becomes a security surface, and harmful inputs can propagate through the system within hours.

Lee, Peter. 2016. Learning from Tay’s Introduction. The Official Microsoft Blog.

4.5.2 ETL and ELT comparison

Batch or streaming decides when records arrive; the next decision is where they are transformed relative to persistent storage. If a pipeline strips out unparsed fields before writing to storage and a later model needs one of those fields, the team must replay months of upstream source logs; if it stores every raw payload first, the storage bill multiplies. Traditional extract, transform, load (ETL)5 (\(\text{E} \to \text{T} \to \text{L}\)) transforms records in an external engine before they land. Extract, load, transform (ELT)6 (\(\text{E} \to \text{L} \to \text{T}\)) lands raw records in staging tables and transforms them inside the warehouse (figure 10). Where that boundary sits determines what each later schema change costs and how much raw data must be retained.

5 Extract, transform, load (ETL): Conventional ETL validates deterministic schema constraints, whereas ML ETL must additionally monitor distributional properties using the statistical drift tests developed in section 4.5.3. A detected shift prompts investigation and validation against labeled outcomes before concluding that model quality has degraded. When feature computation logic changes, reprocessing costs depend on replay scope, windowing, and execution plans rather than requiring an unconditional full-dataset pass.

6 Extract, load, transform (ELT): This approach stores raw data before transformation, allowing teams to revise a transformation query and rerun it over the stored input without re-ingesting from source systems. The iteration time still depends on data volume, query complexity, warehouse capacity, and materialization strategy.

In machine learning systems, the ELT pattern separates ingestion throughput from downstream feature engineering by writing raw, immutable events directly into scalable storage. Keeping those raw events preserves unparsed fields that may later prove useful, so teams can compute new features over historical data without re-ingesting external source streams. When transformation logic changes or a bug surfaces, engineers recompute features by querying the stored raw events rather than replaying upstream logs. The price is physical and operational: raw events take more storage, shared transforms repeat their work unless their outputs are materialized, and keeping unscrubbed raw payloads makes privacy and retention rules harder to enforce. Notebook 4.4 prices these trade-offs on a daily clickstream workload.

Napkin Math 4.4: The cost of transformation placement
Problem: A team processes 10 TB of raw clickstream data daily and must compute user session features for 3 ML models, each requiring different aggregation windows (1-hour, 24-hour, 7-day). Which is cheaper, ETL or ELT?

Accounting boundary: Both designs serve the same aggregation windows and retain replayable upstream raw logs for 30 days. The comparison excludes that shared source retention and its common ingestion costs. ETL retains only the derived history in the warehouse, assumed to occupy 2 TB per model over the same horizon; ELT keeps an additional raw copy there, as when the warehouse cannot query the upstream log store in place, and computes features on demand. Transient query outputs are not retained as separate ELT datasets. Source retrieval and compute for later backfills must be priced separately when the designs differ.

Math:

  1. ETL approach: Transform before loading. Compute all three aggregation windows in a Spark cluster before loading into the warehouse.
    • Spark compute: 10 TB at $5/TB = $50/day
    • Storage: 3 transformed datasets, ~2 TB each = 6 TB at $23/TB/month = $138/month
    • Feature-definition change: Replay the retained source logs and rerun the pipeline; the assumed rerun takes ~4 hours after the edit
  2. ELT approach: Load raw data first, transform in warehouse.
    • Storage: 10 TB raw/day, 30 days retention = 300 TB at $23/TB/month = $6,900/month
    • Query compute: 3 models, each with $5/query of daily query cost over 30 days, totals $450/month
    • Feature-definition change: Rewrite the SQL query (~30 minutes), then execute and validate it against the retained raw copy; execution time is not supplied

Systems insight: ETL saves $6,762/month in storage. After normalizing Spark compute to a monthly cost, ETL totals $1,638/month ($1,500/month compute + $138/month storage), while ELT totals $7,350/month ($6,900/month storage + $450/month query compute), for a cloud-cost advantage of $5,712/month before engineering labor. The other cost is feature-definition change. Compare elapsed time from the start of an edit to validated, feature-ready output in both designs, then account separately for the engineer time spent editing, supervising, and validating. A pipeline rerun and a SQL edit are different endpoints, so their durations cannot establish a speedup or a labor saving. Stable definitions favor this scenario’s lower ETL cloud bill; frequent changes favor ELT only if measured labor and reprocessing savings repay its additional raw copy.

ELT looks expensive here only because this accounting gives it its own raw copy, and raw retention rather than compute dominates the bill. A lakehouse, which runs warehouse queries directly over the stored files, can query the retained logs in place, removing that copy and reversing the comparison. Production ML systems rarely use one pattern exclusively. Structured tables often flow through ETL for efficiency and compliance, while unstructured data pushes the ELT preference further. For deep learning workloads processing images, audio, or text, the “Transform” step often executes inside the ML framework’s data loader rather than in the warehouse at all, applying random crops, spectrogram generation, or text tokenization on-the-fly during each training epoch. Materializing ten augmented variants upstream would increase a 50,000-image dataset to 500,000 stored copies, so ELT’s “load raw, transform late” principle extends naturally to the training loop itself.

Whichever pattern lands the records, integrating diverse sources remains the core ingestion challenge. Data may originate from databases, APIs, file systems, and IoT devices, each with its own format (relational rows, JavaScript Object Notation (JSON)7 documents, binary streams), access protocol, and update frequency. The systems principle is to standardize at the ingestion boundary: normalize formats, validate schemas, and present a consistent interface to downstream processing regardless of source. This boundary standardization separates the complexity of source diversity from the complexity of feature engineering, allowing each to evolve independently.

7 JSON (JavaScript Object Notation): The schema flexibility that makes JSON a common format for APIs creates a validation bottleneck at the ingestion boundary. Unlike binary formats with predefined schemas, every JSON document requires parsing and schema validation before it can be standardized for downstream use. This per-record overhead can make ingestion slower than with binary formats such as Protobuf; the difference depends on the schema, parser, payload, and workload.

In the keyword spotter’s pipeline, backend ingestion takes in activated requests, consented diagnostics, crowdsourced recordings, synthetic data, and validated interactions for serving and training; always-on audio that never triggers the wake word stays on the device. Format normalization and segmentation into consistent durations run as an ETL stage before storage in training-optimized formats, while augmentation stays late-bound in the loader, where ELT’s flexibility pays off, and unparsable captures route to the error-handling path of section 4.5.5.

A record that arrives on time and parses cleanly is well-formed, but well-formed is not the same as trustworthy. A field can parse correctly and still carry a wrong value, a source can drift away from the distribution the model learned, and a label can be wrong while every type check passes. Deciding which records may proceed is the boundary’s next job.

4.5.3 Quality through validation and monitoring

Validation makes that boundary decision, enforcing the quality pillar where a defect is cheapest to stop before it cascades into features, models, and predictions (Sculley et al. 2015). Stopping structural defects is not enough on its own: in a batch of crowdsourced wake-word recordings where a phone app update has silently halved the microphone gain on 15 percent of the clips, every file is still a valid audio file of the right length and sampling rate, so schema checks pass even though the clips are far quieter than anything the detector will hear in deployment. Catching such silent shifts requires pairing schema checks with statistical monitoring across the batch.

Checks at this boundary work at two speeds. A hard failure, such as a source that stops delivering or a batch that fails its schema, must halt the affected path at once. A gradual degradation, such as a feature’s null rate creeping from 1 to 5 percent, a rising duplicate rate, or delivery delays that exceed their service level, is flagged for investigation.
