A global shuffle or a join sends records across every partition, whereas a decomposable reduction such as a mean sends only one partial result per partition. Coordination becomes part of the work, because the machines wait on the slowest worker and must recover when one fails partway through a job. In that case the idempotent, deterministic transformations described earlier become essential rather than merely convenient. Within one machine the network costs disappear and partial failures are rare, so the transformations of this section appear in their simplest form.

The KWS pipeline crosses that cluster boundary only when many experiments rebuild features at the same time: the reference corpus fits on the local storage of one well-provisioned server, and routine development uses single-machine processing on sample subsets to iterate rapidly. Even when feature extraction fans out across workers, per-file extraction is embarrassingly parallel while end-to-end speedup remains bounded by shared storage bandwidth, metadata operations, scheduling, and output contention. At the opposite end of the scale spectrum, production deployment adds a strict 16 KB preprocessing-state budget alongside the 256 KB model-size limit, requiring careful footprint optimization so feature extraction fits on the device.

4.7.5 Feature stores

The keyword spotter needs no feature store, because its features are computed on the device from audio alone. A fraud or recommendation model whose features come from user history faces two failure modes at once. In the first, data scientists compute features like user_purchase_count_30d with batch SQL queries in a warehouse during model development. During serving, an online microservice computes the same metric incrementally from an in-memory cache. Minor differences in timezone arithmetic, missing-value handling, or floating-point rounding cause offline and online features to drift apart. Worse, if the training query joins a historical transaction against the user’s current profile rather than the profile as it stood when the transaction happened, future information leaks into the training set. Keeping the two paths consistent means serving historical feature values for training and current feature values for inference from a single shared definition. Point-in-time correctness and shared feature logic are the primary operational requirements that drive teams to adopt feature stores, which Uber’s Michelangelo platform popularized to eliminate this class of training-serving skew (Hermann and Del Balso 2017).

Hermann, Jeremy, and Mike Del Balso. 2017. Meet Michelangelo: Uber’s Machine Learning Platform. Uber Engineering Blog.
Definition 4.5: Feature store

Feature store is the architectural layer that centralizes the management of machine learning features, decoupling feature computation from consumption.

  1. Significance: It supports point-in-time-correct historical retrieval for training \((\LaggedModelInput)\) and coordinated feature definitions for real-time inference \((\ModelInput_t)\), reducing training-serving skew when timestamps, backfills, freshness, and synchronization are handled correctly.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a general-purpose database, a feature store is designed for dual storage modes: an offline store (columnar/batch) for training and an online store (key-value/low-latency) for serving.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that a feature store is only “a place to store data.” In reality, it manages feature definitions, values, and retrieval; transformation may run in the feature store, an offline store, or a separate batch or stream-compute engine.

Feature stores establish a single source of truth for feature definitions across the ML lifecycle. By registering a feature definition as a unified computation graph, the feature store evaluates the identical transformation across both historical batch data and live inference streams. This shared definition reduces one source of training-serving skew, but equivalent execution still requires synchronized fitted state, correct timestamps, and appropriate freshness. Those conditions must be tested across both retrieval paths. Centralization also enables organizational feature reuse: expensive representations, such as multi-dimensional user embeddings derived from months of interaction logs, are computed once and served to multiple downstream modeling teams.

↪ How feature stores are operated in production, including point-in-time retrieval and training-serving skew prevention at scale, is developed in Feature stores.

To serve both access patterns, feature stores pair two storage engines. The offline store uses columnar formats (such as Parquet) on high-capacity object storage, tuned for multi-gigabyte sequential scans during batch training. The online store uses low-latency key-value databases (such as Redis), tuned for sub-10-millisecond point lookups during real-time inference. Automated pipelines synchronize the two engines by pushing batch-computed feature snapshots to the online store while streaming ingestion updates both stores continuously as new user events arrive.

Time-travel queries separate a true feature store from a simple cache. Training requires evaluating features as they existed at the exact moment of an event, rather than their current values. In a customer churn model, for example, training on a customer who churned on January 15 must use features computed on January 14, not current post-churn profile state. Feature stores enforce point-in-time correctness using an as-of join (temporal join): for every observation at timestamp \(\ObservationTimestamp\), the retrieval engine joins the most recent feature record whose update timestamp satisfies \(\FeatureUpdateTimestamp\le \ObservationTimestamp\). Here \(\FeatureUpdateTimestamp\) must record when the feature value became available in the store rather than merely when the underlying real-world event occurred. If a user action occurred before \(\ObservationTimestamp\) but arrived late over the network or entered through a later backfill, the live serving path at \(\ObservationTimestamp\) could not see it. Excluding records that were not yet ingested at \(\ObservationTimestamp\) ensures that timestamp ordering prevents both training-serving skew and future-information leakage across the upstream feature tables.

Every processing choice in this section, from cleaning and scaling to placement, idempotency, and feature retrieval, leaves derived state that downstream training depends on. Whether features are computed in a loader, materialized in storage, or served from a feature store, the team must eventually be able to say exactly which recordings, labels, and transformation versions produced the dataset a model learned from. Recording that answer is the last step of building the dataset.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An engineer normalizes a numerical feature by computing standard \(z\)-scores: \(x' = (x - \mu)/\sigma\). During production serving, how must the parameters \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) be handled to satisfy the consistency imperative and prevent training-serving skew?

    1. Persist the exact \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) computed on the training dataset alongside the model artifact, loading and applying those fixed constants to live serving inputs.
    2. Recompute \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) dynamically over each incoming serving batch to ensure the live data is always centered at zero.
    3. Discard \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) entirely at inference time and rely on batch normalization layers inside the neural network.
    4. Compute \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) independently over a rolling 1-hour window of serving traffic to track seasonal shifts.

  2. A feature pipeline outgrows one server and its data is split into partitions across several machines. Which operation adds the least network transfer in the cluster compared with running it on one machine?

    1. A join between two tables partitioned on different keys, because the database engine performs the join automatically.
    2. A global shuffle that regroups every record by speaker identifier, because each record moves only once.
    3. Computing a global mean, because each machine can reduce its own partition to a partial sum and count and send only that summary.
    4. Gathering all partitions onto one coordinator machine before any processing, because a single machine avoids coordination entirely.

  3. Define idempotency in the context of data transformation pipelines and explain why idempotent operations (such as upserts) are essential for fault recovery in distributed ML pipelines.

  4. True or False: Using the identical Python preprocessing function in both training and serving code repositories is sufficient to eliminate training-serving skew.

  5. Order the following steps for keeping an audio feature transformation consistent between training and serving: (1) split the data into the appropriate training and evaluation partitions, (2) version the transformation code together with the fitted transformation state, (3) fit any transformation state, such as normalization or vocabulary parameters, using only the training partition, (4) reuse the same transformation or a validated equivalent during serving and check that outputs stay within an accepted tolerance.

  6. How does a feature store’s point-in-time correctness (time-travel join) prevent data leakage during offline training dataset generation?

    1. It encrypts historical feature values so that model weights cannot memorize training labels.
    2. It forces all features to be computed strictly in real time on the client device during model inference.
    3. It converts all timestamps into UTC strings to prevent database indexing errors.
    4. It reconstructs feature values exactly as they existed at the observation timestamp of each training event, preventing future feature values from leaking into historical training records.

See Answers →

4.8 Dataset Release

Months after launch, the keyword spotter’s detection rate drops on a new hardware revision. The first question the team must answer is what the shipped model learned from, down to the recordings, the labels and the guideline they followed, the splits, and the feature code. A dataset release answers that question in advance. It is an immutable, versioned record of everything that produced a training set: the source records, how they were split and labeled, and the code and parameters that turned them into features. It is the versioned dataset that Stage 2 of the lifecycle hands to model development, and it turns the pipeline built so far into something that can be reproduced, compared, and audited.

The release is built from what each record carries. Table 5 lists the fields of one KWS training clip and the question each field answers later. Two of them do most of the work. The split is assigned by hashing the speaker key, so every clip from a speaker lands in the same split in every release and a later relabeling pass cannot leak a test speaker into training. The label carries the version of the guideline it was made under, so a change in labeling rules can be told apart from a change in the data.

Each record’s fields capture local provenance, while the release manifest ties the whole collection together. The rest of a release records how those fields were produced and pins the resulting dataset in storage so that any historical build can be recovered verbatim.

4.8.1 Transformation lineage

The last row of table 5, the feature configuration, exists because recording the raw audio file and label is not enough if a later change to the MFCC window function leaves the team guessing which window the shipped model trained on. Governance, the fourth pillar, closes that gap by tracking what transformations were applied, when they ran, which version of processing code executed, and what parameters were used. This transformation lineage is the processing part of data lineage, the recorded trail that links each dataset, feature, and model to the sources, code, and parameters that produced it.19 Transformation lineage supports reproducibility, debugging, auditability, and iterative improvement when transformation bugs surface. It can supply evidence for documentation or compliance workflows, but lineage alone does not explain a model’s decision or establish regulatory compliance.

19 Data lineage: When a model produces an erroneous or discriminatory prediction, lineage helps engineers determine whether the contributing problem originated in raw data, feature computation, training, or serving. The resulting trace can support incident investigation and jurisdiction-specific documentation duties, but its usefulness depends on complete instrumentation and retained history; it does not guarantee an explanation of model behavior.

That trail starts with transformation versioning, which records which version of the processing code produced each dataset. Whenever engineers change the transformation logic to fix a bug, add features, or improve quality, the version number increments, and tagging datasets with that version identifies every table or shard that must be reprocessed after a fix. Because identical source code can still produce slightly different floating-point outputs across different NumPy releases, runtime environment variables, or CPU instruction sets, each dataset also records the Git commit hash of the code that created it. A container image stored alongside it freezes the code, dependencies, and system libraries in one immutable environment.

Code and environment versions still leave out the data-dependent state that transformations learn. Parameter tracking therefore records the fitted values applied during transformation: the mean and standard deviation computed on training data for normalization, the vocabulary (set of all observed categories) for categorical encoding, and any constants or thresholds used in feature engineering (section 4.7.1). Serializing these parameters alongside the model artifact ensures that serving applies the exact same values as training. Processing lineage then joins code, environment, and fitted parameters into a complete execution history from raw data to final features: which raw files were read, what transformations ran in what order, what parameters were applied, and when the job executed. Lineage systems instrument the pipeline so that each job records these links as it runs, because a history reconstructed after an incident is rarely complete. When model predictions go wrong, engineers can trace back through the lineage graph to find the training data that contributed to the behavior, the quality scores attached to that data, and the transformations applied, then recreate the exact scenario for investigation.

In the KWS pipeline, governance applies to the audio processing parameters that affect model behavior. The reference volume level used for normalization is persisted. The FFT and MFCC settings versioned with the processing code (section 4.7.3) are joined by the window function (such as Hamming or Hann), the frequency range, and the mel filterbank parameters. Recording them makes it possible to reproduce training data exactly when debugging model failures, to validate that serving uses preprocessing identical to training, and to study how preprocessing choices affect accuracy. Without this record, teams fall back on manual documentation that becomes outdated, which allows subtle training-serving skew.

4.8.2 Data versioning for ML reproducibility

Lineage records how a dataset was built, but reproducing or comparing two runs also requires recovering the exact bytes of each release. Without an immutable snapshot of the training inputs, diagnosing why a retrained keyword spotter lost accuracy takes days of guesswork across new recordings, changed labels, and upstream batches. With data versioning, engineers compare the two training snapshots directly and find the cause at once: the labeling guideline version in table 5 changed between the two releases, so the same clips now carry different labels.

File-level versioning engines like DVC (Data Version Control) provide this link by pairing Git with content-addressed remote storage (Iterative 2024). Git tracks small text pointer files (.dvc) containing cryptographic hashes, while DVC synchronizes the underlying binary dataset to object storage (listing 2). Checking out an earlier Git commit restores the historical pointers; the paired DVC retrieval step shown in the listing restores the dataset contents matched to that code state.

Iterative. 2024. Data Version Control (DVC).

While file-level versioning suits bulk artifacts like images and audio, tabular datasets require versioning over tables that change row by row. Open table formats such as Delta Lake solve this by keeping an append-only transaction log of all row insertions, deletions, and schema changes (Armbrust et al. 2020). This log supports time-travel queries that pin a dataset state by timestamp or version ID (listing 3).

Two production components work with these versioning engines to complete end-to-end reproducibility. First, feature store point-in-time retrieval evaluates features as they existed at the observation timestamp, preventing the leakage from future observations described in section 4.1. Second, a model registry records the lineage manifest for each deployed model: Git commit SHA, data version ID, training configuration, and evaluation metrics. In the keyword spotter’s regression, this audit trail turns an open-ended debugging investigation into an immediate snapshot comparison.

↪ How the model registry promotes, holds, or rolls back the exact artifact that was evaluated is developed in Versioning and lineage.

Long-term retention balances auditability against storage cost and privacy rules. High-volume production pipelines use tiered retention: hot SSD tiers for active training and debugging, warm object storage for periodic evaluation snapshots, and cold archive storage for compliance records. Because keeping petabyte-scale data forever is too expensive and often violates privacy statutes, data lifecycle policies must set explicit retention windows and deletion schedules. A release fixes what the dataset is, yet it says nothing about how fast that dataset can reach the accelerator during training. That is the second bound from the opening of this chapter, the one the remaining stages of the pipeline must meet.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In the chapter’s KWS dataset release, each clip’s split is assigned by hashing its speaker key rather than by drawing splits at random each time a release is built. What failure does this prevent?

    1. A later release or relabeling pass could move some of a test speaker’s clips into training, so evaluation would reward memorization of that voice instead of generalization to new speakers.
    2. Hashing compresses the audio so that each split fits in less storage.
    3. Hashing guarantees equal numbers of keyword and non-keyword clips in every split.
    4. Hashing removes the need to record a consent reference for each clip.

  2. A keyword spotter’s detection rate drops after a scheduled retraining even though no code changed. Explain how a dataset release that records label rubric versions, alignment versions, and feature configuration lets the team locate the cause.

See Answers →

4.9 Physics of Data

The throughput bound lives in the Data–Machine overlap, and it is physical. Moving a petabyte of training data across cloud regions incurs tens of thousands of dollars in egress fees and hours of network serialization delays. Source code is small enough to move freely, so the data-as-code principle explains what data does without explaining why moving it costs so much. The physics of data supplies that explanation by giving data measurable properties. Data gravity (McCrory 2010) is the bandwidth, I/O, and latency cost of moving a large byte volume, and task-relevant signal is the learning value those bytes carry for the task. A third property, flow rate, decides whether those bytes reach the accelerator as fast as it consumes them, and the ResNet-50 scenario measures it.

McCrory, Dave. 2010. Data Gravity – in the Clouds.

Returns peak where task signal is high and movement cost (gravity) is low.

Engineering effort pays off most when early filtering and selection separate high-signal records from the rest before data gravity makes them expensive to move. Low-signal data (repetitive audio frames or duplicate samples) consumes host-to-accelerator bandwidth and memory capacity while teaching the model little. An efficient pipeline resolves this physical bottleneck by maximizing the task-relevant signal delivered per byte moved across the storage and interconnect hierarchy, an optimization that begins with the physical properties of the data itself.

4.9.1 Data gravity

Transferring a petabyte dataset across a 10 Gbps link requires days of continuous transmission (\(\DatasetTransferTime = \DataVolume/\Bandwidth \approx 9.3 \text{ days}\)), and even across a dedicated 100 Gbps link, transfer latency and egress tariffs remain substantial enough to govern system design. That physical and economic cost is what the data-gravity principle (2) names: data gravity scales directly with data volume \((\DataVolume)\) and inversely with network bandwidth \((\Bandwidth)\). Because migrating 1 PB across network boundaries is both slow and expensive, system designers colocate compute with the storage substrate rather than moving large datasets across remote networks. This physical constraint underlies lakehouse storage architectures20 (Zaharia et al. 2021), where query and preprocessing engines execute directly against shared object storage rather than repeatedly copying raw data across network fabrics.

20 Data lakehouse: Combines data lake storage (cheap, schema-less) with warehouse query semantics (atomic, consistent, isolated, and durable (ACID) transactions, schema enforcement) using transactional table layers such as Delta Lake. For ML workloads, the lakehouse reduces the ETL copy between lake and warehouse, enabling direct feature computation on the storage layer where data already resides. This is a direct response to data gravity, since repeated petabyte-scale copies increase the \(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\) cost (Armbrust et al. 2020; Zaharia et al. 2021).

Armbrust, Michael, Tathagata Das, Liwen Sun, Burak Yavuz, Shixiong Zhu, Mukul Murthy, Joseph Torres, et al. 2020. “Delta Lake: High-Performance ACID Table Storage over Cloud Object Stores.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 13 (12): 3411–24. https://doi.org/10.14778/3415478.3415560.
Zaharia, Matei, Ali Ghodsi, Reynold Xin, and Michael Armbrust. 2021. “Lakehouse: A New Generation of Open Platforms That Unify Data Warehousing and Advanced Analytics.” Proceedings of CIDR 8.

↩︎ The data-gravity principle, which weighs shipping a dataset against shipping the computation to it, is established in Cloud infrastructure and scale.

Napkin Math 4.7: The physics of data gravity
Problem: A 1 PB training dataset resides in a US East data center, while a remote accelerator pod is available in US West. What transfer time and egress cost does remote training add, and when would suitable compute near the data avoid that overhead?

Math:

  1. Network bandwidth: 100 Gb/s \(\div 8 =\) 12.5 GB/s.
  2. Transfer time: 1,000,000 GB \(\div\) 12.5 GB/s = 80,000 seconds, approximately 22 h.
  3. Cost: 1,000,000 GB \(\times\) $0.02/GB = $20,000. (Baseline: AWS data transfer out pricing, 2024.)

Systems insight: If training takes less than 22 h, data transfer takes longer than training. If training costs less than $20,000 (approximately 5,000 TPUv4-hours), bandwidth costs more than compute. When suitable compute is available near the data, these transfer costs favor moving code to data. Smaller datasets make moving data to code more practical, but placement still depends on compute availability and comparative training time.

4.9.2 Task-relevant signal

A dataset containing one million identical images carries heavy data gravity yet adds no new observations beyond the first sample; repeated passes over that single image can nudge model weights slightly, but they supply no fresh evidence. Conversely, a curated collection of 10,000 diverse edge cases is small enough to move cheaply yet contributes rich supervisory signal. Physical storage measures raw bytes, whereas machine learning extracts task-relevant signal: evidence that helps the model learn the target task rather than raw Shannon entropy. Comparing task-relevant signal with data gravity motivates a selection heuristic: \[ \text{Data Selection Gain} \propto \frac{\text{Task-Relevant Signal}}{\text{Data Gravity}} \tag{3}\] The numerator is not a universal physical quantity. A team estimates marginal learning value by comparing held-out model quality under a fixed training procedure; the denominator accounts for the movement cost of the compared datasets. The heuristic favors a selection only when its measured learning benefit justifies that cost. Scoring, labeling, and training costs must also enter a complete budget. Deduplication and the active learning of section 4.6.5 can improve this ratio by eliminating redundant byte volume and proposing uncertain examples for labeling. Uncertainty does not establish learning value; ambiguous or corrupted examples can also be uncertain, so the selection policy requires validation.

↪ The information-compute ratio, which measures the learning signal each sample returns per unit of training compute, is developed in The information-compute ratio.

4.9.3 The feeding problem

Data gravity dictates the cost of moving data in bulk; the feeding problem dictates the cost of delivering it to execution units in real time. It is an input/output flow rate bottleneck: storage bandwidth fails to keep pace with compute throughput. The bottleneck is measured, not hypothetical. Profiling nine widely used vision and audio models found their training time often dominated by data stalls, time spent waiting for data to be fetched and preprocessed (Mohan et al. 2021). In production recommendation training, the data storage and ingestion pipeline has become a dominant constraint on training performance and capacity (Zhao et al. 2022).

Zhao, Mark, Niket Agarwal, Aarti Basant, Buğra Gedik, Satadru Pan, Mustafa Ozdal, Rakesh Komuravelli, et al. 2022. “Understanding Data Storage and Ingestion for Large-Scale Deep Recommendation Model Training.” Proceedings of the 49th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture, 1042–57. https://doi.org/10.1145/3470496.3533044.

When data delivery and computation overlap, the bottleneck principle holds that a system runs only as fast as its slowest stage. Consider the chapter’s throughput workload, one A100-class accelerator training ResNet-50 on images. The accelerator’s peak arithmetic rate is 312 TFLOP/s, and one training step on one image costs about 24.6 GFLOP, roughly three times the cost of a single prediction because training must also work out how to adjust every parameter. Dividing the first by the second gives an arithmetic ceiling of about 12,682 img/s. In practice a well-tuned pipeline sustains about 3,200 img/s, roughly 25 percent of that ceiling, once layers that wait on memory and the synchronization between steps take their share. Each image sits on disk as a 150 KB JPEG, so storage must deliver 480 MB/s. When the attached volume supplies only 250 MB/s, compute units stall on I/O. This gap defines the feeding loss, the throughput penalty from insufficient input supply, which directly degrades system efficiency \((\EfficiencyHw)\). Under steady-state overlap, divide the supplied 250 MB/s by the required 480 MB/s to obtain the fraction of the sustained training rate that storage can support. Subtracting that fraction from one gives a modeled feeding loss of about 48 percent. This is the throughput loss relative to the supplied sustained baseline, not a measurement of all accelerator idle time or utilization relative to advertised peak.

↩︎ The bottleneck principle is established in The bottleneck principle.

Which bytes are counted matters as much as how many. After decoding and conversion to an FP32 tensor, the same image occupies 602 KB, about 4 times its compressed size, so a loader that converts on the host moves 1.9 GB/s through host memory and across the host-to-accelerator link. Many loaders ship 8-bit pixels instead and convert on the accelerator to avoid that cost. Compression thus trades storage bandwidth for CPU decode work. A pipeline that caches pre-decoded tensors on disk to skip that work pays the full 1.9 GB/s at the storage tier instead, and it freezes the random crops that augmentation would otherwise vary from epoch to epoch. Applying the same supply-to-demand calculation gives a modeled throughput loss of 87 percent relative to the sustained baseline. Storage and delivery apply this slowest-stage bound to the storage tiers (section 4.10.3) and then to the CPU decode workers (section 4.10.4) without re-deriving it. Distance also increases energy consumption: as data travels farther from arithmetic logic units, transport energy dwarfs the cost of computation itself.

Systems Perspective 4.2: The energy cost of data movement
The memory-wall analysis in Physical Constraints on Deployment implies a data-engineering principle: moving one 32-bit value costs roughly 173× to 10,811× as much energy as the table’s FP32 multiply, with the multiplier rising as the data leaves the chip. The DRAM ratio is smaller than the one in Iron Law of ML Systems only because that comparison set one byte against a cheaper FP16 multiply. Later optimization chapters exploit the same cost gradient inside model and hardware design. Here, the question is how much energy the information flow from the external world already consumes before the model computes, at each level of the memory hierarchy (table 6).

That gradient is why data locality dominates systems optimization. Each avoided transfer eliminates its corresponding movement energy, compounding across training epochs.

Because data movement incurs physical energy costs, data volume acts like physical mass. Removing 50 percent of training data through deduplication saves more than persistent storage capacity. It also eliminates energy expenditure across network, memory, and preprocessing stages. Filtering uninformative samples early is among the most effective architectural levers when I/O and communication dominate workload runtime.

Horowitz, Mark. 2014. “1.1 Computing’s Energy Problem (and What We Can Do about It).” 2014 IEEE International Solid-State Circuits Conference Digest of Technical Papers (ISSCC), 10–14. https://doi.org/10.1109/isscc.2014.6757323.
Checkpoint 4.3: The physics of data

Data engineering is governed by physical costs. Check your intuition:

The energy hierarchy and the signal-density heuristic converge on the same optimization lever, the Data Selection Gain of equation 3. Every redundant or uninformative record removed early saves movement energy and interconnect bandwidth at each later stage without costing task-relevant signal. For the released dataset, these costs come due in delivery. Every training epoch moves it from storage, through the host, to the accelerator, and the feeding problem decides whether that movement keeps pace.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A machine learning team maintains a \(1\text{ PB}\) raw training corpus in a US East cloud storage bucket and provisions a dedicated compute cluster in US West. The regions are connected by a dedicated \(100\text{ Gbps}\) network fabric. Cloud egress pricing is $0.02/GB, and the model training run takes \(20\text{ hours}\). Under the chapter’s data-gravity reasoning, which conclusion is best supported by these numbers?

    1. The team should stream the dataset remotely during training, because a \(100\text{ Gbps}\) link guarantees that data movement cannot become the bottleneck.
    2. The team should split the dataset evenly across regions, because cross-region placement automatically removes the need to evaluate transfer cost or placement constraints.
    3. The team should first apply compression, because any realistic compression plan will make the transfer clearly cheaper and faster than colocating compute.
    4. The ideal one-time transfer is about \(22.2\text{ hours}\) and costs about $20,000, so colocating or relocating compute near the data is worth evaluating, but the final decision still depends on compute availability, cost, and other deployment constraints.

  2. Assume decoded tensors must be read from storage at a sustained demand of \(1.9\text{ GB/s}\), while the attached baseline block-storage path can supply only \(250\text{ MB/s}\). Assume no compression or cache alternative changes this bound. Under this simplified feeding-loss model, what follows?

    1. The storage bound reduces the sustained baseline throughput by about \(87\%\) relative to its supplied demand, because the supply-to-demand ratio is \(0.25/1.9 \approx 0.132\) and the feeding loss is about \(1-0.132 \approx 0.87\).
    2. The accelerator remains compute-bound, because storage throughput and tensor execution are independent once batches have been scheduled.
    3. Increasing batch size alone removes the bottleneck, because larger steps always improve the ratio between storage supply and decoded-tensor demand.
    4. The storage path is only modestly underprovisioned, so the waiting fraction must stay below \(20\%\).

  3. Using the data selection gain relationship \(\text{Gain} \propto \frac{\text{Task-Relevant Signal}}{\text{Data Gravity}}\), explain why removing \(50\%\) redundant samples can have large systems value when the retained dataset still covers the task-relevant cases and the training schedule is held fixed in passes over the data. Why is quality validation still necessary?

  4. According to the chapter’s energy-movement hierarchy, arrange the following operations in ascending order of energy consumed per 32-bit value (from lowest energy to highest energy):

  1. Local NVMe SSD access
  2. On-chip 32-bit FP multiply
  3. Data-center network transfer
  4. Off-chip DRAM memory access

4.10 Storage and Delivery

Delivery is where the throughput bound is met or missed. On baseline cloud block storage, the ResNet-50 scenario of section 4.9.3 already loses about 48 percent of its sustained training rate. Following that scenario to its end means choosing a storage tier that can sustain the accelerator’s rate, keeping the host processor between storage and accelerator from becoming the next bottleneck, and cutting the bytes each sample costs to move. The KWS data poses the same questions at smaller scale, for the multilingual reference corpus (23.4 million samples spanning 50 languages) and for the much smaller product-specific subset.

A sub-millisecond point lookup fits only in DRAM or local NVMe.

4.10.1 Storage system options

The ResNet-50 job reads its images in long sequential scans, while a serving system fetches one feature vector per request. These opposing access patterns pull storage in two directions, and selecting a system means minimizing the data term \(\left(\frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth}\right)\) of the iron law of ML systems for whichever pattern dominates. Every storage medium imposes physical constraints on bandwidth that determine the maximum speed of the training and serving pipelines.

Two storage performance metrics govern this optimization. IOPS (input/output operations per second) counts the distinct read or write requests a device can handle per second, bounding random access workloads such as fetching small batches of images or individual user profiles. Throughput (bandwidth) measures the volume of data transferred per second, typically \(\text{IOPS} \times \text{Block Size}\), bounding sequential access workloads such as scanning a Parquet file for training.

Three architectures cover these patterns, and the ResNet-50 scan and the feature-serving lookup sit at opposite ends of the range. Conventional databases (online transaction processing (OLTP)) optimize for high IOPS with small, row-oriented blocks, strict ACID guarantees, and sub-millisecond point lookups. That profile fits online feature serving, such as a recommendation system fetching one user profile or an anti-fraud service querying historical transaction counts, but it wastes bandwidth on a training scan. The storage engine fetches entire rows even when the model consumes only 20 of 100 features. Data warehouses (online analytical processing (OLAP)) organize tables by column rather than by row (Stonebraker et al. 2018), so a sequential scan reads only the required features, transferring one fifth of the uncompressed row payload when selecting 20 out of 100 columns. The format-efficiency calculation in section 4.10.5 quantifies this gain with a worked fraud-detection scenario. Warehouses suit feature engineering, batch aggregation, and tabular training, but they require structured schemas, handle raw audio, imagery, and free-form text poorly, and make schema changes during experimentation costly ALTER TABLE operations across billion-row tables. Data lakes store structured, semi-structured, and unstructured data in native formats on high-capacity object storage and defer structure to query time, an architectural pattern known as schema-on-read.21 A ResNet-50 job can read raw JPEG files from a lake at sequential rates, while independent jobs impose their own schemas without coordinating migrations. The cost is that governance shifts from ingestion to retrieval. Without a metadata catalog recording lineage, update cadence, schema versions, and access policies, a lake degrades into a “data swamp,” a set of orphaned directories such as userdata_v2_final and userdata_v2_ACTUALLY_FINAL whose lineage and quality are unrecorded. Each storage architecture therefore optimizes for a distinct access pattern and lifecycle phase, as table 7 summarizes.

Stonebraker, Mike, Daniel J. Abadi, Adam Batkin, Xuedong Chen, Mitch Cherniack, Miguel Ferreira, Edmond Lau, et al. 2018. “C-Store: A Column-Oriented DBMS.” In Making Databases Work: The Pragmatic Wisdom of Michael Stonebraker. Association for Computing Machinery. https://doi.org/10.1145/3226595.3226638.

21 Schema-on-read: Applies data structure definitions at query time rather than during ingestion, contrasting with schema-on-write (traditional databases) where data must conform to a predefined structure before storage. For ML pipelines in early development, schema-on-read enables rapid experimentation, because teams can store raw sensor data, images, and logs without committing to a feature schema upfront.

Each lifecycle stage also reads the same data in its own way: exploratory analysis samples it at random, training streams it sequentially at high throughput, and production serving issues low-latency point lookups. During initial development, iteration velocity and schema flexibility dominate raw throughput. The primary systems challenge is managing experiment revisions without exploding storage capacity: ten exploratory runs on a 100 GB dataset naively generate 1 TB of redundant copies. The metadata-pointer approach of section 4.8.2 avoids this by decoupling version tracking from file storage. Governance policies reinforce this tiering by granting broad access to anonymized development datasets while gating sensitive production data behind encrypted, auditable access tiers.

During training, the access profile shifts toward sustained sequential throughput. Modern deep neural networks process large datasets across dozens or hundreds of epochs, making storage bandwidth the determinant of hardware utilization. Scaling the chapter’s ResNet-50 scenario to eight accelerators, each sustaining its per-device rate, gives 25,600 img/s and a sustained storage demand of 3.8 GB/s of 150 KB compressed images; staging decoded FP32 tensors instead would raise it to 15.4 GB/s. Storage tiers unable to sustain this rate idle the accelerators, inflating cluster operating costs. End-to-end ResNet-50 training cannot shrink that demand by precomputing features, because its features change at every step. A model trained on the outputs of a frozen upstream encoder (a pretrained model whose outputs are reused as input features) can, and there the feature placement trade-off (section 4.7.2) becomes critical. Precomputing those representations delivers a 30× storage reduction (150 KB raw inputs reduced to 5 KB embedding vectors) but introduces staleness when the encoder updates.

Production inference reverses the optimization target from sequential bandwidth to low-latency random access. A recommendation service handling 10,000 req/s with a 10 ms SLA and 10 feature reads/request demands 100,000 IOPS. Local Non-Volatile Memory Express (NVMe)22 storage can sustain this random-access rate within the latency budget; in-memory key-value stores such as Redis, distributed caches, and purpose-built feature stores buy headroom for tail latency. Edge deployment imposes further physical boundaries: constrained flash storage, intermittent network connectivity, and non-disruptive model updates dictate tiered caching, where core models reside in local flash while reference data synchronizes asynchronously with cloud endpoints. In production, serving infrastructure must support atomic rollbacks and parallel A/B testing, operational guarantees that depend on tracing every deployed model back to the exact dataset release from which it was trained. Production ML architectures therefore rarely select a single storage technology; mature systems combine all three, with databases serving point lookups, warehouses running feature extraction, and lakes holding raw captures and intermediate checkpoints.

22 NVMe (Non-Volatile Memory Express): A storage protocol connecting directly to the PCIe bus with 64K command queues, delivering 5–7 GB/s sequential throughput and microsecond-scale latency. The contrast with SATA SSD (500 MB/s, single queue) is a 10× bandwidth gap that can bottleneck a training pipeline when storage service time exceeds compute time.

4.10.2 Storage tiers and cost

Beneath each of these systems sits a physical storage medium, and media span roughly two orders of magnitude in cost and nearly four in bandwidth. Mismatching the storage tier to the access pattern either inflates infrastructure costs with underutilized capacity or starves training accelerators during I/O stalls.

That spread survived a long collapse in absolute price. Figure 18 traces the lowest recorded price per terabyte of each medium in constant 2020 dollars. Hard disk storage fell from about $88B per terabyte in 1956 to $10.6 in 2023, roughly 10 orders of magnitude, yet in 2023 DRAM still cost 42× as much as solid-state storage per terabyte, and solid-state storage cost 2.4× as much as hard disk. Capacity became cheap, but since the 1980s the gaps between tiers have not closed, so the choice of tier, rather than raw capacity, sets the cost of keeping a training job fed.

ML systems use tiered storage because no single tier is both cheap and fast (table 8). Under these 2024 assumptions, storing the KWS reference corpus (748.8 GB) in object storage costs $17.2/month, enabling affordable raw-audio retention, while working datasets on NVMe cost $74.9/month–$224.6/month for active training but load 50× faster.

↪ Why no single medium offers both large capacity and low access latency, the reason storage is built in tiers, is collected for reference in The memory hierarchy.

In wall-clock terms, that 50× bandwidth gap changes the input-loading component of engineering iteration time on the same KWS corpus: loading at 5 GB/s on local NVMe completes in 149.8 s, compared to 7,488 s over a single object-storage stream. That loading speedup translates into shorter experiments only to the extent that exposed I/O contributes to their duration, since transformation and model execution still consume time.

Beyond input datasets, storage must hold what training produces: model parameters, the extra values training keeps for each parameter (its optimizer state), and periodic checkpoints of both. Modern neural networks contain millions to hundreds of billions of parameters, imposing storage and retrieval patterns distinct from tabular data. GPT-3 (Brown et al. 2020) requires approximately 700 GB for model weights when stored in FP32 format (175B parameters times 4 bytes), though practical deployments often use smaller numeric formats such as FP16 (350 GB) to halve memory pressure. Model weights require block-aligned storage formats to support parallel reads across parameter groups during initialization and checkpointing, demanding aggregate storage bandwidth approaching network fabric limits (25 to 100 Gbps) to keep compute units active.

Brown, Tom B., Benjamin Mann, Nick Ryder, Melanie Subbiah, Jared Kaplan, Prafulla Dhariwal, Arvind Neelakantan, et al. 2020. “Language Models Are Few-Shot Learners.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 1877–901. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2005.14165.

↪ Quantization, which shrinks these weight footprints by storing parameters at lower precision, is developed in Numerical Precision: Changing How Values Are Stored.

Tracking these artifacts introduces versioning challenges. Git’s incremental text diffs fail on multi-gigabyte binaries, where a single weight update produces an entirely new file and ten historical checkpoints of a 10 GB model occupy 100 GB. Production workflows therefore keep lightweight content hashes in Git and the binaries in content-addressed object storage, the same decoupling that section 4.8.2 applies to datasets.

Multi-accelerator training compounds storage pressure through concurrent I/O. When a training job spans multiple accelerators, every rank reads training batches concurrently, and the job periodically writes checkpoints of model and optimizer state, often from a single rank, in bursts that compete with those reads. Storage must absorb reads that scale with the worker count and checkpoint bursts on top of them, preventing I/O serialization that leaves expensive accelerators idle.

↪ The data- and model-parallel strategies that coordinate these workers within one node are developed in Single-node multi-GPU training.

4.10.3 The storage bandwidth budget

Each storage tier sets its own ceiling on the feeding problem of section 4.9.3. Host RAM delivers 50 to 200 GB/s on modern server nodes, local NVMe SSDs deliver 5–7 GB/s, and network-attached storage provides 1 to 10 GB/s. Whichever tier serves the training reads caps the data supply rate, so the design question is which tier can sustain the chapter’s ResNet-50 scenario, and at which of its two rates.

Napkin Math 4.8: Storage bandwidth budget
Problem: What storage configuration keeps an A100-class accelerator from being data-starved while training ResNet-50? Performance Bounds explains why delivered throughput falls short of an accelerator’s advertised peak; here, the point is how a compute ceiling becomes a data-path budget.

Start with the compute ceiling derived in section 4.9.3. The A100’s 312 TFLOP/s divided by about 24.6 GFLOP per training image gives 12,682 img/s.

That compute ceiling becomes a storage requirement once each image must arrive from disk or object storage. Storage reads compressed bytes, so the budget uses the 150 KB JPEG rather than the decoded tensor: feeding the ceiling takes approximately 1.9 GB/s, while the 3,200 img/s that the accelerator actually sustains takes 480 MB/s.

Three storage options can now be compared against these two targets:

  • S3 Standard delivers about 100 MB/s per connection when it streams large objects such as sharded archives, so feeding the ceiling needs 19 concurrent worker threads before software overhead. Fetching each 150 KB image as its own object is latency-bound and delivers far less.
  • SATA SSDs deliver about 500 MB/s sequentially, enough for the sustained rate but a bottleneck at the ceiling.
  • NVMe SSDs deliver approximately 5–7 GB/s, which covers the ceiling with headroom.

Systems insight: A SATA SSD caps the pipeline at 500 MB/s divided across 150 KB images, or approximately 3,333 img/s. That clears the sustained 3,200 img/s, so a faster drive buys nothing at today’s rate. Once faster kernels or a faster accelerator push the rate toward the ceiling, the same drive holds an A100-class accelerator to 26 percent of it. Each fix to the slowest stage exposes the next, so the storage tier must be sized for the rate the pipeline is heading toward, not the rate it runs at today.

Designing for high-throughput training therefore starts by matching storage throughput to accelerator demand, the storage-tier form of the slowest-stage bound that section 4.9.3 measured as lost training rate. The 500 MB/s figure represents effective SATA III sequential read throughput (the interface ceiling is 550 MB/s), and real-world random read performance with small files can be significantly lower. That caveat reinforces the general principle governing data pipelines: equation 4 bounds training throughput by compute capacity and the data supply rate defined in equation 5. Let \(\TrainingSampleThroughput\), \(\ComputeLimitedSampleRate\), and \(\DataSupplyRate\) denote rates in samples per second; let \(\StorageBandwidth\) be storage bandwidth in bytes per second, \(\OperationalOverheadFraction\) the dimensionless fraction lost to overhead, and \(\SampleStorageSize\) the bytes per sample. Under full overlap, the min-of-rates form corresponds to the \(\TrainingStepTime= \max(\IronLawComputeTime, \StepIoTime)\) lower bound of section 4.9.3, which the dataloader section applies to host workers; with incomplete overlap, actual step time is higher. \[\TrainingSampleThroughput\leq \min(\ComputeLimitedSampleRate, \DataSupplyRate) \tag{4}\] \[\DataSupplyRate= \frac{\StorageBandwidth(1-\OperationalOverheadFraction)}{\SampleStorageSize} \tag{5}\]

Once \(\DataSupplyRate\) is the smaller rate, additional accelerator compute cannot raise training throughput; the remedy must increase usable storage bandwidth or reduce bytes per sample. The budget assumes every epoch reads from storage. A dataset that fits in host memory, as an ImageNet-sized dataset does on many eight-accelerator servers, is read from storage once and then served from the operating system’s page cache, so storage binds only on the first epoch. The budget governs the cases that remain: datasets larger than memory, nodes whose memory several jobs share, and data streamed from remote storage.

When storage bandwidth becomes the limiting factor, teams must either improve storage performance through faster media, parallelization, or caching, or reduce the amount of data that must move. Large language model training may require processing hundreds of gigabytes of text per hour, while computer vision models processing high-resolution imagery can demand sustained data rates exceeding 50 gigabytes per second across distributed clusters. Prefetching hides the time to fetch the next batch behind the computation on the current one, but only when the loader sustains the required supply rate; it cannot create bandwidth that storage lacks. Provisioning a fast drive, however, solves only the first half of delivery, because compressed bytes from storage must still be decoded and transformed by the host processor before the accelerator can use them.

4.10.4 The dataloader

Between storage and the accelerator sits the host processor, which reads each stored example, decodes it, augments it, and stages it for transfer. The barrier at this stage is the input/output (I/O) bottleneck: the accelerator idles whenever the host cannot stage examples as fast as the accelerator consumes them. The slowest-stage bound of section 4.9.3 applies unchanged: in an idealized two-stage model, step time ranges from \(\max(\IronLawComputeTime, \StepIoTime)\) with full overlap to \(\IronLawComputeTime + \StepIoTime\) with no overlap. What changes is the binding resource: while the initial feeding scenario was starved by baseline storage bandwidth, data loading on provisioned storage is more commonly starved by CPU-side decompression and decode (Mohan et al. 2021).

Mohan, Jayashree, Amar Phanishayee, Ashish Raniwala, and Vijay Chidambaram. 2021. “Analyzing and Mitigating Data Stalls in DNN Training.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 14 (5): 771–84. https://doi.org/10.14778/3446095.3446100.
Murray, Derek G., Jiřı́ Šimša, Ana Klimovic, and Ihor Indyk. 2021. “Tf.data: A Machine Learning Data Processing Framework.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 14 (12): 2945–58. https://doi.org/10.14778/3476311.3476374.

In computer vision, JPEG decode and augmentation on host CPUs represent the typical binding stage; in keyword spotting, MFCC extraction and audio augmentation load the same host threads. This host-side preparation executes within the framework’s data loader (such as PyTorch’s DataLoader), which decodes and transforms raw records across parallel worker processes before staging batches for device transfer. Input-pipeline frameworks such as tf.data treat this stage as a system in its own right, with operators for parallel transformation, prefetching, and caching (Murray et al. 2021). To isolate CPU worker scaling, assume storage and bus transfers are provisioned to satisfy the 480 MB/s compressed input requirement, which a SATA solid-state drive does (section 4.10.3). When storage and interconnect are nonbinding, the training pipeline depends entirely on whether CPU workers can match the accelerator’s sustained rate of 3,200 img/s. If each worker process transforms 375 img/s, 9 workers are required to reach the capacity crossing. Below this threshold the CPU input pipeline binds, and adding accelerators yields no speedup, which surprises teams that expect compute investments to scale linearly. Above the crossing, launching additional workers produces no gain because the accelerator binds. The worker count represents a capacity match rather than a hardware constant: actual ResNet-50 requirements on a given accelerator shift with image transformations, host processor architecture, batch size, and runtime configuration. Figure 19 plots this capacity crossing, where the starved regime gives way to a compute-saturated plateau.

At eight accelerators the worker count grows eightfold, more decode cores than many hosts can spare. Relieving the choke point takes three mechanisms. Multi-process worker pools scale decoding across host cores. Asynchronous prefetching copies the next batch to the accelerator while it computes on the current one. Hardware decode engines move image decompression off the host processor entirely. Hardware decode removes the host bound and hands the constraint back to storage bandwidth, where the remaining lever is the number of bytes each sample costs to move.

↪ How asynchronous prefetching and overlapping pipeline stages hide host preparation latency behind accelerator compute is developed in Data prefetching and overlapping.

4.10.5 Formats, compression, and partitioning

Scanning a 100-column CSV file when the model uses only 20 columns wastes four-fifths of disk bandwidth on unused text, and opening millions of individual 150 KB JPEG files spends more time on filesystem seeks than on transferring bytes. File format sets the byte count and access pattern, so format selection directly scales the data term \(\left(\frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth}\right)\) of the iron law. Columnar formats like Parquet excel for tabular features by allowing column projection and pushdown filtering, whereas shard-based sequential formats like WebDataset or TFRecord pack millions of small binary objects (such as images or audio) into contiguous sequential streams. This sharding eliminates random disk seek overhead (\(\Latency\)), turning slow random I/O into sustained sequential storage throughput (\(\StorageBandwidth\)). The price is shuffling. Records within a shard arrive in order, so loaders approximate a random order by shuffling the shard list and mixing records in a buffer, and too small a buffer feeds the model correlated batches. Redesigning the format and the loader together pays off at the scale of this chapter’s lighthouse. One such system trains ResNet-50 on ImageNet to 75 percent accuracy in about 20 minutes on a single machine (Leclerc et al. 2023). Quantifying this impact as format efficiency \((\FormatEfficiency)\) provides a multiplier on effective bandwidth. While Parquet optimizes columnar layout on disk, in-memory representations face a similar serialization bottleneck when passing data between ingestion engines, dataframes, and ML frameworks. The Apache Arrow standard solves this in-memory boundary by defining a language-independent columnar memory layout, enabling zero-copy data sharing across pipeline processes without expensive serialization and deserialization cycles.

Leclerc, Guillaume, Andrew Ilyas, Logan Engstrom, Sung Min Park, Hadi Salman, and Aleksander Mądry. 2023. “FFCV: Accelerating Training by Removing Data Bottlenecks.” 2023 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 12011–20. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr52729.2023.01156.

↪ Illustrative CPU decoding throughput and deserialization cost across storage formats are collected for reference in Serialization cost.

Napkin Math 4.9: Format efficiency
Storage formats determine how much “waste” data must move to retrieve the signal needed for training. Let \(\FormatEfficiency\) be the dimensionless fraction of bytes read that carry selected features, from 0 to 1. Equation 6 shows how that fraction scales physical bandwidth into usable pipeline bandwidth: \[ \text{Effective Bandwidth} = \text{Physical Bandwidth} \times \FormatEfficiency \tag{6}\] Values below 1 mean the format spends part of the physical data path on irrelevant bytes. Improving storage layout can therefore raise usable throughput without changing the underlying storage device.

Scenario: Training a fraud model using 20 features from a 100-column table, assuming equal uncompressed byte size per column.

Row-oriented formats (such as CSV) must read all 100 columns to retrieve the 20 required features, giving a useful-byte fraction of 0.2 and wasting 80 percent of disk bandwidth. Conversely, column-oriented formats (such as Parquet) read only the selected columns, achieving \(\FormatEfficiency\approx\) 1 (ignoring metadata overhead) and delivering 5× higher effective throughput.

Systems insight: In this projection-only scenario, switching from CSV to Parquet provides the same effective read-throughput gain as a 5× faster data path.

Columnar storage formats such as Parquet or Optimized Row Columnar (ORC) achieve a 5\(\times\) to 10\(\times\) I/O reduction for typical ML workloads through two complementary mechanisms: column projection, and column-level compression that exploits repeated values within a column. Compression is particularly effective for categorical features with few distinct values. A country code column with 200 unique values across 100 million records compresses 20\(\times\) to 50\(\times\) through dictionary encoding, while run-length encoding compresses sorted columns by storing only value transitions. Together, projection and compression cut I/O by 20\(\times\) to 100\(\times\) compared to uncompressed row formats, shortening training epochs and shrinking storage footprints.

↪ How row and columnar layouts arrange records on disk, and how projection pushdown reads only the selected column chunks, are collected for reference in Row and columnar formats.

Compression algorithms trade storage density against CPU decompression bandwidth. While gzip achieves higher compression ratios of 6\(\times\) to 8\(\times\), Snappy achieves 2\(\times\) to 3\(\times\) compression but decompresses at 500 MB/s, roughly 4.2× faster than gzip’s 120 MB/s. Reading a 100 GB dataset compressed with gzip costs 13.9 minutes of CPU decompression per epoch, while Snappy costs only 3.3 minutes. Over 50 epochs, this 10.6 minutes difference per epoch compounds to 9 hours of host CPU time. That CPU time lengthens training only when decompression sits on the critical path, as it does when host-side decode is the binding stage of the dataloader choke point. With spare host cores and prefetching, it hides behind accelerator compute. The trade reverses when storage bandwidth binds instead. Gzip’s higher compression ratio means fewer bytes per sample cross the storage path, which raises \(\DataSupplyRate\) in equation 5, so the right codec depends on which stage binds. Snappy suits a CPU-bound loader, and a denser codec suits a storage-bound one.

Beyond format and compression, data partitioning governs retrieval efficiency by aligning storage layout with common access patterns. The KWS corpus can be partitioned by speaker and recording date, so a training run can select a slice without scanning the whole corpus, and the speaker key that assigns splits also governs the storage layout. Partitioning strategies interact directly with distributed training. Range partitioning by user ID routes each user’s records to one data-parallel rank (workers that each train on a different slice of every batch), which feeds each rank a skewed stream rather than a representative sample. Random partitioning mixes records from the source pool across workers under the sampling policy. It does not remove correlations already present in that pool or prevent leakage between evaluation splits; speaker grouping and other independence constraints must be enforced separately. Granularity matters: too few coarse partitions limit parallel worker concurrency, while millions of tiny shards add heavy metadata overhead and break sequential disk prefetching. In multi-accelerator training, partition skew creates stragglers. If seven workers in an eight-accelerator job finish loading their batch in 12 ms but one worker reads from an oversized partition and takes 180 ms, that single straggler delays the synchronization step that ends each training step across the entire cluster. Balanced partitioning that matches worker read budgets therefore keeps accelerator efficiency high.

Format, compression, and partitioning complete the delivery path by cutting the bytes each sample costs to move and keeping worker shards balanced. With a storage tier sized for the accelerator, the host matched to its rate, and bytes per sample reduced, both scenarios now have every stage of the pipeline in place, and each can be checked against the targets it started with.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An ML systems architect must select storage backends for three distinct workloads: (1) Millisecond point lookups of user feature vectors during real-time online serving; (2) High-throughput sequential scans over tabular fraud features during batch training; (3) Storing petabytes of raw, unstructured multi-modal audio and video recordings. Which mapping of storage architectures to workloads is optimal?

      1. Low-latency transactional database / key-value store; (2) Columnar data warehouse; (3) Scalable cloud data lake (object storage).
      1. Cloud object storage (S3); (2) Key-value database; (3) Columnar data warehouse.
      1. Columnar data warehouse; (2) Cloud data lake; (3) Low-latency transactional database.
      1. Scalable cloud data lake; (2) Low-latency transactional database; (3) Columnar data warehouse.

  2. Explain the storage-bandwidth bottleneck when feeding accelerators directly from cloud object storage versus local NVMe SSDs, and describe the common architectural caching pattern used to resolve it.

  3. True or False: In a columnar storage format like Apache Parquet, reading 10 columns out of a 100-column table requires scanning the entire uncompressed row payload from disk.

  4. Order the following release-preparation steps for a storage design that must support both model training and online serving: (1) validate point-in-time training retrieval together with serving freshness and latency, (2) define the training and serving access requirements, (3) materialize the appropriate offline training path and online serving path, (4) fix the versioned feature definitions and fitted transformation state.
